### Foreword

I never camped outdoors with my family, nor did I ever camp when I was a cub scout. The I only things of note from that time in my life are that my den mother was a former Miss Michigan and my father and I enjoyed designing and crafting pinewood derby cars. When I did start camping a few years later, I quickly took to it with great zeal. I enjoyed getting out in the woods with just my gear in my backpack to rely on. Evenings in a tent meant playing games, including a fair number of RPG sessions. RPGs involve storytelling, adventure, mystery, and suspense—all core features of my time in scouting, which continues to this day. When I applied to be a counselor for the Game Design merit badge, I simply listed “Chaosium” in the qualifications section!

Nowadays, I continue to enjoy camping, especially when there’s an opportunity to build a campfire each evening. Building a fire provides a timeless, comforting, and social experience regardless of the weather. Many of my fondest memories as a scout feature a campfire under the stars, the songs we sang, the ghost stories we told, and the skits we laughed at. My favorite scouting skills involve teaching how to gather tinder, kindling, and wood to construct and ultimately light a campfire. When I became my troop’s scoutmaster a little over a decade ago, I swore an oath that I would only light a campfire using flint and steel, and so far, I have honored my promise, even when deterred by rain and snow! If nothing else, it’s a core survival skill that I hope I never _really_ need to use.

As a scout, I learned how to cook, canoe, hike, navigate with a map and compass, forage for food, sail a boat, tie knots, make shelters, identify poisonous plants and dangerous animals, and many other skills that could certainly come in handy as a _Call of Cthulhu_ investigator. You find a different and deeper appreciation for the world when you are out in the wilderness. The night sky is darker and full of more stars, while the sounds are more primitive and sometimes startling. You develop a stronger connection to nature as you ponder its mysteries, its power, and its eternal indifference to our wants and needs. It’s somewhat spooky to hear things like thunder in the distance or the snap of a twig down the trail, or just what creature is staring at you with those glinting eyes from out of the darkness.

I wish I could have experienced the early days of scouting back in the 1920s and 1930s. As a collector of scouting memorabilia, I ponder what stories the items I obtain might tell if they could speak. Being rather nostalgic, the simpler technology, the slower pace of life, and a more rural society all these items harken back to appeals to me. Until time travel becomes possible, I’ll happily settle for playing the scenarios in this book, and enjoying the stories that unfold from them at the gaming table— or possibly at a picnic table on a campout with my troop. And yes, I always have a set of dice in my pack!

—Yours in Scouting, Rick Meints Scoutmaster, Troop 4, and President of Chaosium.


> — INTRODUCTION


### Preface: the Story of This Project

Normally, I would not spend ink describing the genesis of a project; however, it is a cute story and hopefully, worth a read. While my sons were in the Boy Scouts of America[®] (as I had been), I enjoyed volunteering and being a part of their journey. It allowed me to get back to nature and activities I enjoy, such as hiking and camping. A highlight for the boys was a week at scout camp in northern Wisconsin. On one of those early camp outings, I volunteered to go with the troop and be an in-camp leader for the week.

Now, I had known that several of the boys (including my own) enjoyed playing tabletop roleplaying games and collectible card games. I thought _Call of Cthulhu_ might be fun; it’s easy to learn, and it would allow them to participate in their own ghost story while in the woods. I wrote a fun but deadly oneshot scenario (an early version of **Tremors Below** , page 70) and rolled up investigators. I decided to make the story outdoor and scout-themed, and the investigators, scouts (many feature later in this book).

The last night, we gathered in the dining hall at a large table. I ran them through the scenario, enthralling everyone for hours. We finished sometime after 11 pm, and the walk through the woods to our campsite was quiet. The next day, the children told me how much they had been freaked out on the way home! I thought, _“Oh crap, I am going to get some angry calls from parents.”_ Instead, they were hooked, and I continued to write new adventures for camp and cabin outings over the years. Those scenarios became the basis for this book.

### —joshua Hoyt

### Introduction

What is it about listening to a spine-tingling ghost story told around a fire in the deep, dark woods that amplifies the scary sensations? What makes the hairs on the back of your neck stand up as a sense of dread builds? Is it simply the cadence of the storyteller and the suspense of the tale? Or is it something much deeper and darker?

Our ancestors huddled around fires for protection, not just to stave off the cold, but also to keep predators away. If you have ever lain awake in a tent, imagining every sound to be some monster about to strike, you have felt that same primal fear.

This book provides an opportunity to experience _Call of Cthulhu_ in a new way: one that helps keep the game fresh and challenges both the Keeper and players to adapt and find new opportunities to roleplay—the true heart of cooperative storytelling games. While the Cthulhu Mythos is woven into the fabric of these stories, it is not the driving force. Many of these scenarios operate more like ghost stories or the setup to a high-action horror movie.

This book allows you to play a junior investigator, full of wild-eyed naïveté and innocence, rather than the usual grizzled veteran. The investigator creation guidelines and backgrounds provided help craft a character who just stepped out of a Norman Rockwell* painting. For anyone who camped growing up, this taps into those primal fears found only in the wild.

* _Normal Rockwell (1894–1978) became famous for his folksy paintings of US life that appeared on the covers of the_ Saturday Evening Post, _but he also made many scoutthemed ones._

### What’s in This Book?

This book contains four linked scenarios that form the Westhaven Campaign. The scout-investigators start the first scenario aged 11, and reach adulthood in the final chapter. At the end, the scout-investigators advance to adult investigators, ready to embark on adventures and campaigns beyond Westhaven— such as the epic _Call of Cthulhu_ campaigns, _Masks of Nyarlathotep_ or _A Time to Harvest_ .

The scout squad is a new investigator organization for _Campfire Tales_ , and the bonds they make within their squad will never be forgotten.

### Content Warning

_Call of Cthulhu_ is a game about horror and mystery, and scenarios may feature such things as evil cults, deadly creatures, graphic violence, gore, death, rotting corpses, and untrustworthy authority figures, among other horrors.

Also, sadly, countless cases of abuse have taken place within various scouting organizations around the world. It would be remiss of us not to acknowledge and condemn such acts. Such things do not feature in this book.

While some groups may want to explore a more visceral, gritty game world, others may desire a subtler approach and avoid certain matters. We recommend Keepers adjust their presentation of this subject matter in accordance with their own and their group’s comfort level. Some excellent free “RPG safety toolkits” are available online—we recommend checking them out to see if they are a good fit for you and your players.

_Campfire Tales_ presents Keepers and players with new tools such as:

- Rules for creating junior investigators—a host of new “hobbies” (occupations) and backstory elements.
- Assisted fighting maneuvers—allowing scoutinvestigators to combine forces in combat.
- Adversity—testing the scout-investigators’ resolve in the face of hunger and fatigue.
- Scout ability badges—each provides a unique in-game bonus.

### Inspiration

Beyond the obvious inspiration found in the real Scout Movement, additional fuel for creativity can be found in other areas. Literature, television, and movies brim with plucky, ragtag groups of youths who go on adventures, face danger, and triumph. One early example from cinema was the _Our Gang_ crew of poor children; these features were among the first to show white and Black children interacting as equals.

Starting in the late 1920s, _The Hardy Boys_ and _Nancy Drew_ books presented wholesome American youth that investigated mysteries—sometimes of a seemingly supernatural cause. While the antagonists were almost always human, the air of mystery and suspense added tension. Between the two, over 300 books appeared in the series.

A big hit on Saturday morning television, _Scooby Doo, Where Are You?_ began in 1969, created by HannaBarbera Productions. This cartoon series featured teenagers Fred Jones, Daphne Blake, Velma Dinkley, Norville “Shaggy” Rogers, and their talking Great Dane named Scooby Doo.

The group solved mysteries involving supposedly supernatural creatures through a series of antics and missteps, while driving around the country in the Mystery Machine (their iconic customized van). While the adventures always teased supernatural creatures, the culprit was always some ne’er-do-well in a rubber mask, lamenting that they _“would’ve gotten away with it if it wasn’t for you meddling kids.”_ Movies and television continued to use these themes, often presented as a story that was a throwback to an earlier era. The journey of a young boy who befriends a stranded alien is chronicled in _E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial_ (1982). _The Goonies_ (1985) follows a group of kids who attempt to save their homes from foreclosure; in the process, they discover a treasure map that leads them to the lost fortune of One-Eyed Willy, a 17[th] -century pirate. _Stand by Me_ (1986), based on Stephen King’s novella _The Body_ , followed four friends’ journey to see a corpse and their resulting coming-of-age adventures.

_Super 8_ (2011) told the story of teenagers filming their own movie when a train derails, releasing a dangerous presence into their town. The story is set in 1979 with heavy period influences. The story plays out like a much scarier version of _E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial,_ but also focuses on themes of loss and regret. More recently, _Stranger Things_ (2016–present) has become a world-wide sensation. Set in the fictional town of Hawkins, Indiana, during the 1980s, the protagonists are a group of kids (who happen to like roleplaying games!). Season One focuses on the investigation into a young boy’s disappearance amid supernatural events, including the appearance of a girl with strange powers.

### History of Scouting

This book’s scenarios present a fictional scouting organization to which the scout-investigators belong. The history of the real-world Scout Movement goes back over 100 years. In that time, it has changed, evolved, and spread across the globe. While the Scout Movement is old, many of its influences are even older—by 150 years or more. It continues in various forms to this day.


### Historical Note

Many of the cinema and stories referenced here often portrayed racial and ethnic stereotypes that are considered problematic by today’s standards—a theme common across much early media and daily life. We included them as historical examples of the genre of youth adventures (early young adult) that this book contains.

### Early Youth Movements

The Young Men’s Christian Association (YMCA) was founded in 1844 by George Williams (1821–1905) and his colleagues. The Industrial Revolution brought many young men to the growing cities. Williams and his cohorts were concerned about the lack of healthy activities for young men in major cities—the most prominent options of the day were usually brothels and taverns. Williams’ idea grew out of prayer meetings he held among his fellow workers in the City of London, England. This led to the founding of the YMCA with the stated purpose of _“the improving of the spiritual condition of young men engaged in the drapery, embroidery, and other trades.”_ William Henry Harrison Murray (1840–1904) authored a series of articles and books about the Adirondack Mountains in upstate New York. He was an ordained minister, delivering sermons in Connecticut and Massachusetts that focused on the outdoors and the Adirondacks. Murray wrote of the north woods of New York as being _“health-giving and spirit-enhancing.”_ He admired the Adirondack woodsmen and became known as “Adirondack Murray”—the father of the Outdoor Movement.

One of the first modern dedicated youth programs was the Woodcraft Indians, founded by Ernest Thompson Seton in 1901. Under Seton’s leadership, the group co-opted Native American myths and iconography (or at least, stylized misconceptions common in that era) to teach its message. Originally for boys, it later expanded to include girls.

Seton instructed the children in outdoor skills (or woodcraft, as it was known then). The program spread
quickly across the globe; and it strongly influenced later youth programs including the Sons of Daniel Boone (founded by Daniel Carter Beard in 1905), the Scout Movement, and the Lone Scouts (founded in 1913 by John Hargrave).

### Scouting’s Founding in England

The founder of the Scout Movement was an Englishman named Robert Baden-Powell. His formative years were spent at Charterhouse, one of England’s most famous public schools (an elite, feepaying institution). While there, his interest in the outdoors took hold and grew. Later, Baden-Powell served as a military officer in the British Army. He was stationed in India (then a part of the British Empire) during the 1880s, and it was in this period that he took a keen interest in military scouting. The lessons he learned culminated in a publication titled _Reconnaissance and Scouting_ (1884).

During the Second Matabele War in Southern Rhodesia (present-day Zimbabwe), Baden-Powell served as chief of staff to Gen. Frederick Carrington. It was during this time that Baden-Powell began a lifelong friendship with Frederick Russell Burnham. Burnham was the chief of scouts for the British Army in Africa at the time. Burnham had been born and raised on a reservation in America to white and Dakota parents. Baden-Powell worked with Burnham on many reconnaissance missions into enemy territory. Many of Baden-Powell’s later ideas concerning the Boy Scouts originated during this period.

Burnham introduced Baden-Powell to woodcraft, which was largely unknown outside of its main practitioners in the US West, including frontiersmen and the indigenous peoples. Often, they discussed the changing nature of warfare in Africa and the need to develop training programs for young soldiers. The tenets of this program would be rooted in woodcraft principles: exploration, tracking, fieldcraft, and self-reliance.

A few years later, Baden-Powell was stationed in the town of Mafeking in present-day South Africa during the Second Boer War. The British troops in Mafeking were under siege by a much larger Boer force for over a year. During that time, the Mafeking Cadet Corps was formed. This freed adult soldiers to concentrate on military duties and provided a positive outlet for the town’s boys. The performance and success of the Cadet Corps inspired Baden-Powell’s later Scout Movement.

Baden-Powell’s Mafeking efforts also made him an unexpected celebrity in the UK where newspapers

### Baden-powell

Robert Baden-Powell, like many famous founders of organizations, US presidents, and sports heroes of the era had problematic views on race, religion, sexual orientation, socio-economic class, and ethnicity by today’s standards. The sections below document the organizations’ history and the positive benefits received, rather than lionize the individual.

followed his exploits. When the siege was broken, Baden-Powell became a national hero. This popularity caused sales of _Aids to Scouting_ —an instruction book written in 1899 with Burnham’s aid—to soar. When Baden-Powell returned, he saw many teachers and youth organizations using _Aids to Scouting_ .

Baden-Powell later corresponded with Ernest Thompson Seton. In 1906, Seton sent Baden-Powell a copy of his 1902 book, _The Birch-Bark Roll of the Woodcraft Indians_ . They met later that year to discuss ideas for what characteristics were important for a successful youth training program. Afterward, in 1907, Baden-Powell authored the first draft of his program in a book called _Boy Patrols_ . Baden-Powell gathered 21 boys from private London-area schools and held a week-long camp on Brownsea Island. The boys organized themselves into small units using BadenPowell’s draft patrol system.

In 1908, he published _Scouting for Boys_ as a six-part series (later collected into one book). Baden-Powell reworked the _Aids to Scouting_ concepts extensively; he removed the military aspects, focusing on woodsmen and explorers as role models rather than soldiers in demonstrating outdoor techniques. Educational aspects of the program were also expanded, and the writing focused on more age-appropriate themes. The new book enjoyed even greater success than _Aids to Scouting_ . Soon after, boy patrols appeared all over England, using Baden-Powell’s book as inspiration.

Some 11,000 Boy and Girl Scouts attended the first scout rally, held in London, 1909 (girls were included early). Baden-Powell retired from the army in 1910. Originally, he intended his book for use by other organizations, like the Boys Brigade. However, his popularity, and requests for guidance from scouts all over England caused him to form a new organization, The Boys Scouts Association, and later, The Girl Guides. By 1911, The Boy Scouts Association boasted
over 100,000 scouts. Specialized units were later added to the program including the Sea Scouts and Air Scouts.

### Export to United States (the Bsa[®] )

The progressive movement of the early 20[th] century saw more families shift from rural farming areas to the cities, primarily for greater economic opportunities. This caused some concern that US boys were potentially losing important aspects of their lives: most notably, self-reliance, patriotism, and America’s famed individualism. Several organizations sought to fill that gap including the YMCA, Woodcraft Indians, and the Sons of Daniel Boone.

The saga behind the founding of the Boy Scouts of America[®] (BSA[®] ) has deeply imprinted itself upon the organization’s values.

According to the lore, in 1909, Chicago-based publisher William D. Boyce was visiting London on business when he became lost in a pea-soup fog. While seeking directions, Boyce encountered a boy who helped him navigate the city’s foggy streets. When Boyce attempted to pay the boy, the boy (later referred to as the Unknown Scout), politely declined; he replied that he was a Boy Scout and simply doing a good deed.

Boyce was so impressed he met with staff at the Boy Scouts Association headquarters to learn more. When he returned to the US, he founded the Boy Scouts of America[®] .

The Girl Scouts of the United States of America was founded by Juliette Gordon Low in 1912. Low met Robert Baden-Powell on a trip to England in 1911; she was so impressed with the education provided by the Scout Movement that she brought her version to the US. She started in her hometown, Savannah, Georgia, and it became instantly successful.

### Scouting in Other Countries

Encouraging youth to achieve their full potential, become model citizens in a non-political, educational program without exclusion of race, gender, or socioeconomic background became a powerful 20[th] -century rallying cry. Soon after the founding of the scouting associations in England and the US, similar organizations sprang up around the globe. While many original themes persist, each has taken their own distinct paths, especially regarding co-education.

Although some countries continue separate organizations for boys and girls, other countries, especially in Europe, have merged into one mixedgender organization.

### Goals of Scouting

The worldwide programs collectively referred to as the Scout Movement have unique distinctions based on locale. But they also have similarities. Skills development, respect and care for the natural world, citizenship, and leadership factor into all of them. While some view the scout uniform as “militaristic,” it also ensures that all scouts dress equally, regardless of social or economic standing.

The BSA[®] ’s primary objectives include character development, citizenship, personal fitness, and leadership. These goals are part of an age-appropriate educational system known as the Scout Method. These ideals appear in the Scout Oath, Scout Law, Outdoor Code, Scout Motto (“Be Prepared”[®] ), and slogan (“Do a good turn daily”). Boy Scouts™ share responsibilities, such as cooking and cleaning while camping; and they learn skills like the patrol method, which organizes members into small groups with their own youth leadership. Adults observe, provide role models, and mentor; youth leaders provide skills education and hands-on leadership by example.

Girl Scouts focus on empowerment and leadership, and promoting ideals such as citizenship, confidence, courage, compassion, and entrepreneurship. Also, they emphasize community service and practical skills, such as camping and first aid. Their most famous entrepreneurship activity is the eponymously named “Girl Scout Cookies[®] ” peddled every year by tenacious US girl scouts.

### Wayfarer Scout Oath

As a Wayfarer Scout, I promise to display scout values daily and pledge always to:

- Conduct myself with integrity and honor.
- Be an exemplary citizen of my country and the world.
- Be a thoughtful steward of the environment.

### The Wayfarer Scouts

This book uses a _fictional_ scouting organization named the Wayfarer Scouts, inspired by real scouting groups worldwide.

The Wayfarer Scouts were founded in 1918 by Reginald Corker; Corker grew up in a small farming community outside of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania. Corker’s frequent hunting, fishing, and hiking trips with his father impacted the lad profoundly. They imprinted a lifelong love for nature on the boy.

Captain Corker, as his peers called him, was an experienced US soldier and Great War veteran. He served on the Western Front and at the First Battle of Cambrai, where he fought bravely and received medals and honors. He subsequently invalided out of the army and returned home in 1918 to settle in Arlington, Virginia with his wife, Gertrude. Corker continued to consult with the US Army on training techniques while also taking teaching positions at area colleges.

During the war, Corker came to understand the plight of the young men who fought and died. Some were barely old enough to shave. Many, clearly overwhelmed by the war’s ferocity and horror, could not adapt. So many of these unprepared souls ended up face down in the mud of Flanders Fields, never to return home. Corker realized, if he could find a way to better prepare these young men, fewer would die on the battlefield.

So, Corker ran his idea past business, military, and academic leaders. He sought the brightest minds in developing a program that would deliver his vision. Those discussions led to the formation of the Wayfarer Scouts; a non-profit organization dedicated to preparing the leaders of tomorrow.

His military experience and Pennsylvania adventures convinced him the teaching platform for his organization should be the natural world, wild places especially. As a result, Corker focused the Wayfarer Scouts on outdoor leadership. He believes almost all important lessons that young people need could be learned by getting out into nature. Helping people measure themselves against the harsh, unpredictable wilderness, while also acquiring useful skills, was a challenge he embraced.

At the same time, Corker was the father of three young girls. Progressive socially, he wanted his
daughters to grow into strong, independent women. His wife, Gertrude, supported the suffrage movement, campaigning for women’s right to vote, and her views played a role too. As a result, Corker made the Wayfarer Scouts mixed gender from day one. His time on the battlefield also taught him important lessons about race—a soldier’s courage did not come from the color of their skin. Consequently, he opened the Wayfarer Scouts to all youth, regardless of their background or ethnicity.

The message and experiences of Captain Corker’s new organization resonated with the US youth. By 1919, thousands of boys and girls were participating in the program. This early success led to the development of scout camps where Wayfarer Scouts could spend weeks improving wilderness and leadership skills. By 1925, the numbers had swelled to over 250,000.

### Organization

The primary organizational structure in the Wayfarer Scouts is the squad. A squad is made up of four to eight scouts who meet regularly to practice skills, earn advancement, participate in service opportunities, or just go adventuring. Squad members may be assigned different responsibilities, but no command structure exists as they are encouraged to cooperate and work together. Each squad chooses a name and a yell for their group. Animal names are common, such as the Red Elk Squad, Sidewinder Squad, or the Gray Wolf Squad (with a squad yell of “Owwww-oooooo”).

A collection of squads linked together by geography, such as a local community, is known as a unit. Adult volunteers oversee each unit, providing guidance, teaching, and advice. The adult in charge is known as the Wayfarer Leader. Other adults who support the leader are called Assistant Leaders. They offer advice and sometimes accompany squads or units on camping trips into the wilderness. Currently, one unit is in **Westhaven** (see **Chapter 3** ). This is Unit #104, which most call the “Westhaven Unit.”

Wayfarer Scouts progress through the program in a structured manner. As scouts learn and demonstrate new skills, they are promoted to higher ranks. Once
a scout turns 18, they age out and most likely move on to higher education, employment, or military service. Some scouts continue or return as adult leaders within the Wayfarer Scouts.

As Captain Corker likes to say, _“A Wayfarer Scout can be easily spotted by their good works.”_ In practice, when Wayfarer Scouts work in the community, they typically wear their uniforms. Wayfarer Scouts wear a heavy, dark-green cotton shirt adorned with numerous badges signifying their rank, skills they have acquired, and adventures they have been on.

Headgear is not required. Scouts that can afford one wear a brimmed felt hat with the Wayfarer Scout logo pin; others wear a simple brown beret.

### Wayfarer Ranks

A Wayfarer Scout may earn the following four ranks during their journey:

as a Wanderer, regardless of age. Youths 11 and older are eligible to join. Wanderers often suffer from homesickness on their first few camping trips; older scouts can help ease the transition. For promotion, Wanderers should demonstrate proficiency in the following:

- Citizenship
- First aid (Basic)
- Hiking basics
- Knot tying (Basic)
- Map and compass

### Rover

A Rover has demonstrated higher levels of skills and knowledge. They should have spent at least 21 nights camping outdoors with their unit, and at least one week-long scout camp. For promotion, the Rover must also demonstrate proficiency in the following:

- Axe, saw, and knife safety

### Wanderer

These newest members of the Wayfarer Scouts are typically the youngest, although all Wayfarer Scouts start

- Cooking (Basic)
- Fire building
- First aid (Advanced)
- Camping techniques


> — INTRODUCTION

### Ranger

The Ranger is an experienced Wayfarer Scout and shows a high level of proficiency in all basic skills. They must demonstrate knowledge of the following:

- Cooking (Advanced)
- Edible and poisonous plants
- Fire-building using primitive techniques
- Orienteering (Advanced)
- Wilderness survival

In addition, the Ranger should participate in 15 hours of community service. The Ranger must demonstrate the skills they have accumulated with a solo camping trip; for this, they must plan the gear and food they should bring and select the location. They must hike there and camp alone for one day and night. This typically serves as the last step before Warden.

### Warden

Wayfarer Scouts who successfully navigate the lower ranks earn the rank of Warden. They are typically the oldest, most experienced scouts.

Wardens have demonstrated a strong grasp of the various skills needed by a Wayfarer Scout, camped many nights, and displayed strong leadership skills, including directing at least three community service projects. The Warden teaches younger scouts and passes on their knowledge. Wardens passing all final skill challenges receive the title of Chief Warden, the highest award a Wayfarer Scout can attain.

The Wayfarer Scouts live by a motto of “Be a kind person today.” Their charge is to do a good deed daily. This can be anything from holding the door for someone to shoveling snow for an elderly neighbor. At each meeting, Wayfarer Scouts pledge to uphold the Wayfarer Scout Oath in their daily lives (see **Wayfarer Scout Oath** , page 10).

### The Westhaven Campaign

This book’s four scenarios can be played as one-shots or in sequence to form the Westhaven Campaign. Assuming campaign play, have players create scoutinvestigators of 11–12 years old (Wanderer rank) or pick from the **Pre-Generated Scout-Investigators** (page 174). Play can then begin with the first scenario, **Tremors Below** (page 70).

Each scenario is set two years apart, one for each scouting rank (Wanderer, Rover, Ranger, Warden), and the scout-investigators should age and develop between adventures (see **Scout-Investigator Development Phase** , page 47).

Feel free to incorporate additional scenarios at any stage. **Scenario Seeds** (page 165) are included with this in mind.

As the final scenario opens, Shadow Over Westhaven (page 132), the scout-investigators are 17–18 years old, their scouting days coming to an end.

### Beyond Westhaven

On reaching adulthood, the scout-investigators are ready to become adult investigators (see **Coming of Age: Becoming an Adult Investigator** , page 49), prepared to take on a myriad of _Call of Cthulhu_ scenarios and campaigns.

The adventures of their youth should stand them in good stead, especially as they share the common bond of having been in the same scout squad—a bond that constitutes a unique Investigator Organization (see _Call of Cthulhu: Investigator Handbook_ , page 122).


### Chapter 1: Creating Scout-investigators

This chapter provides rules for creating scout-investigators. Alternate investigator T creation methods are at the end.

While investigator creation remains largely the same as standard _Call of Cthulhu_ , important customizations exist. Characteristics and skills should reflect a young person rather than a fully formed adult. Backstory is important, especially when creating a group that all know each other.

### The Steps

The five steps to creating a scout-investigator follow:

- **Step One: Generate Characteristics** (nearby).

Write the results on the scout-investigator sheet, filling in the half and fifth values alongside the full characteristic percentage:

- Divide the percentage by two, rounding down, for the “half” (Hard) value.
- Divide the percentage by five, rounding down, for the “fifth” (Extreme) value.

**Note:** the half and fifth numbers are used in relation to characteristics and skills in the game. We recommend calculating these up front to ease play. If using the writable PDF version of the scoutinvestigator sheet, it automatically calculates those values for you.

- **Step Two: Determine Hobby** (page 17).
- **Step Three: Decide Skills & Allocate Skill Points** (page 23).
- **Step Four: Create a Backstory** (page 23).
- **Step Five: Gear & Possessions** (page 28).

### Step One: Generate Characteristics

Use the following rolls to determine each characteristic:

Then roll for Strength (STR), Size (SIZ), and Education (EDU) as dictated by the age of the character, as follows. If playing these scenarios in order, start as an 11- to 12-year-old (Wanderer) scout-investigator.

### _**scout-investigators Aged 11–12 Years (wanderer)**_

- Strength (STR): roll 2D6+ 3, then multiply the result by 2.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 3, then multiply the result by 3.

- Constitution (CON): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 5.

- Dexterity (DEX): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 5.

- Appearance (APP): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 5.

- Intelligence (INT): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 5.

- Power (POW): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 5.

- Education (EDU): roll 2D6+ 3, then multiply the result by 2.

### _**scout-investigators Aged 13–14 Years (rover)**_

- Strength (STR): roll 2D6+ 3, then multiply the result by 3.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 3.

- Education (EDU): roll 2D6+ 3, then multiply the result by 3.


### Chapter 1

### _**scout-investigators Aged 15–16 Years (ranger)**_

- Strength (STR): roll 2D6+ 4, then multiply the result by 4.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 5, then multiply the result by 4.

- Education (EDU): roll 2D6+ 4, then multiply the result by 4.

### _**scout-investigators Aged 17–18 Years (warden)**_

- Strength (STR): roll 2D6+ 4, then multiply the result by 5.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 4, then multiply the result by 5.

- Education (EDU): roll 2D6+ 4, then multiply the result by 5.

### Determine Other Attributes

- Luck: roll 2D6+ 6 and multiply by 5.

- **Cool:** equal to POW.
- **Hit points (HP):** add CON and SIZ together, then divide the total by 10 (round down).

***Note:** Sanity points (SAN) are not used in _Campfire Tales_ (see **Cool** , page 43).

### Family Credit Rating

A scout-investigator may earn pocket money, but their general affluence is a product of their family. Family Credit Rating determines two backstory elements that are likely to be relevant in play: their home and possessions. Consider these when imagining the scout’s homelife; check your ideas with the Keeper, then make entries under their backstory for “Home” and “Gear and Possessions.”

Family Credit Rating implies the occupations of the scout-investigator’s parents or legal guardians. It can also create conflict and story. For example, “ **Bob” Harley** (page 57) might not take kindly to penniless kids in his candy shop, whereas he smiles and greets wealthy ones.

- **Magic points (MP):** equal to one-fifth of POW.
- **Damage Bonus (DB)** and **Build:** add STR and SIZ together and look up the total on **Table 1: Damage Bonus & Build** (nearby).
- **Movement Rate (MOV):** compare DEX, STR, and SIZ as per **Table 2: Movement Rates** (nearby).

### Table 1: Damage Bonus & Build


| STR | + | SIZ | Damage | Bonus | Build |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 25 – 64 –2 –2 |
| 65 – 84 –1 –1 |
| 85 – 124 None 0 |
| 125 – 164 +1D4 1 |


### Table 2: Movement Rates

|**Compare DEX, STR, & SIZ**|**MOV RATE**|
|---|---|
|If both DEX and STR are less than SIZ|MOV 7|
|If either STR or DEX is equal to or greater than SIZ, or if all three are equal|MOV 8|
|If both STR and DEX are each greater than SIZ|MOV 9|


_Examining Clues_
### _**roll to Determine a Scout-investigator’s Family Credit Rating (d100):**_

- 01: **Penniless**
- 02 – 15: **Poor**
- 16 – 85: **Average**
- 86 – 99: **Wealthy**
- 100: **Rich**

### Penniless

Always hungry and forced to fend for themselves. They are the victim of unfortunate circumstances; clearly, something has gone seriously awry not only for them, but also for society for a child to live in such poverty.

- **Home:** no fixed home, they perhaps lodge with a friend or relative, or are provided for by the state or charity.
- **Possessions:** nothing but the rags they currently wear. Their pockets are empty.

### Poor

Often hungry and rely on handouts. The adults in their lives struggle to provide, perhaps due to illness, disability, or lack of work.

- **Home:** basic. Most likely overcrowded or vacant because everyone is out looking for work.
- **Possessions:** dressed in hand-me-downs. May own a battered old bicycle. They have some string in their pockets.

### Average

They may not have everything they want, but they have everything they need.

- **Home:** a large, comfortable house with a car parked outside. Some domestic help (cleaners, cooks, gardener, etc.). A second place in the country (such as a hunting lodge). Someone works in a higher-end white-collar profession (bank manager, police chief, accountant, etc.)
- **Possessions:** a shiny bicycle, nice clothes, and a wristwatch. They have something they can show off in their pocket, such as that baseball card everyone wants (signed, of course).

### Rich

These kids go to expensive private schools, but may end up mixing with everyone else as a Wayfarer Scout. Try as they might to blend in, their dress, hair, accent, and vocabulary likely give them away.

- **Home:** a large comfortable house set behind a wall and ornate gates. The chauffeur sometimes escorts them. Someone might own a large company or be a member of an influential, affluent family.
- **Possessions:** shiny stuff and lots of it. Their pockets contain a scented handkerchief and two ten-dollar bills.

### Step Two: Determine Hobby

The hobby categories below follow the notion of “what I want to be when I grow up;” many use the adult occupational counterparts as models. A scoutinvestigator may join the Junior Firefighter Brigade because they want to have a career in firefighting when they grow up. The newspaper carrier may wish to become a journalist, while the science club member may aspire to be a scientist.

- **Home:** a comfortable place to live, it may be big or small; tidy or untidy; busy or quiet; whatever it is, it’s home. Someone is employed as a bluecollar professional (skilled laborer, electrician, mechanic, etc.) or lower-end white-collar profession (schoolteacher, police officer, bank clerk, etc.)
- **Possessions:** a bicycle, all necessary school clothes and supplies. In their pockets, they have a pocketknife, some candy, and a folded-up comics’ page.

### Hobbies

These are only a sample of the options available. Feel free to work with your Keeper to add more (see **Creating Hobbies** , nearby).

- Amateur Sleuth
- Cheerleader
- Farmhand
- Junior Actor
- Junior Artist

### Wealthy

A step above average; kids from wealthy backgrounds may attend the same schools as the kids from average backgrounds.

- Junior Firefighter
- Junior Musician
- Junior Photographer
- Junior Police Corps
- Junior Sailor

- Library Helper
- Nature Enthusiast
- Newspaper Carrier

Each hobby includes a brief description, followed by three entries: **Obligations** (page 28); **Trusted Adult** (page 25); Skills (see **Step 3** , page 23), and badge (see **Ability Badges** , page 30). Once you choose your scout-investigator’s hobby, write it down on the investigator sheet.

**Note:** not all skills are listed on the scoutinvestigator sheet. If a chosen hobby includes skills that are not featured on the scoutinvestigator sheet, simply write the skill on one of the lines at the end of the skill list.

### Creating Hobbies

Just like in regular _Call of Cthulhu_ , players can work with the Keeper to create hobbies.

- Choose eight skills associated with the hobby.
- Identify a trusted adult skilled at the hobby.
- What obligations go along with the hobby, if any?
- Which scout badge best sums up the hobby’s skills?

### Amateur Sleuth

The amateur sleuth finds crime and detective stories fascinating. They never leave without their trusty magnifying glass and notebook, and have a keen ear for rumors.

- **Obligations:** none.
- **Trusted adult:** librarian, local historical society, local reporter.
- **Skills:** one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Law, Library Use, Listen or Spot Hidden, Locksmith or Psychology, Stealth, Track, any other one skill as personal or era specialty.

### Cheerleader

Making the cheerleading squad requires fitness and dedication. The competition is fierce. Cheerleaders should know a lot about their team and the sport. As they progress, they may help train newer recruits.

- **Obligations:** training practice and attendance at matches and parades.
- **Trusted adult:** sports teacher and coaches, local
- sports people.
- **Skills:** Climb, Dodge, Jump, Throw, Spot Hidden, any three other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Climbing Badge** (page 31).
- **Badge:** starts with the **Reading Badge** (page 33).


### Creating Scout-investigators

### Farmhand

If raised on a farm or in a rural community, the scoutinvestigator may well find themselves “helping out.” Such work is likely to be low or unpaid and can be physically demanding. Harvest (July to September being the peak times in the US) is an especially busy period when all hands are required.

- **Obligations:** as and when required on the farm.
- **Trusted adult:** local farmers and farm workers, farm supply shops.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Farming), one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Mechanical Repair, Natural World, Track, any three other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Animal Friendship** , **Fishing** , or **Nature Badge** (pages 30, 31 and 32).

### Junior Actor

This budding thespian loves serious drama, classics, or musicals. They appear in every school drama club production and attend drama camp every summer. Those who can afford it make regular trips to the local theatre for both stage and screen performances. Wealthy families might see shows in Boston or go to the Ziegfield Follies in NYC.

- **Obligations:** regular rehearsals and shows.
- **Trusted adult:** drama teacher, local amateur dramatics groups, actors, and patrons.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Acting), Disguise, History, two interpersonal skills (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Psychology, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Public Speaking Badge** (page 32).

### Junior Artist

The young artist always has a sketchbook and paint under their fingernails. They spend lunch breaks in the school art room. They may aspire to be a painter, graphic artist, or cartoonist. They understand diagrams and sketch maps well.

- **Obligations:** none.
- **Trusted adult:** art teacher, local artists.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (any), History or Natural World, one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Language (Other),

Psychology, Spot Hidden, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.

- **Badge:** starts with the **Crafting Badge** (page 31).

### Junior Firefighter

The junior firefighter dreams of joining a fire department to fight fires and be a first responder. They may spend hours at a local fire station, help with crowd control, or volunteer in the first aid tent at community events.

- **Obligations:** regular afterschool sessions, attendance at occasional events.
- **Trusted adult:** doctors, nurses, local business owners, local clinics.
- **Skills:** Climb, Dodge, First Aid, Mechanical Repair, Spot Hidden, any three other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **First Aid Badge** (page 31).


_Junior Musician_
### Junior Musician

The young musician attends private lessons and rehearsals with the school band, orchestra, or chorus. They may sing, play instruments, or both. Most likely, they perform in a large ensemble. They may also compose.

- **Obligations:** music lessons, performances, practice, and rehearsals.
- **Trusted adult:** local musicians, music teacher.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Instrument), one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Listen, Psychology, any four other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Ability Badge** of your choice (page 30).

### Junior Photographer

Photography as art has been around a long time. By the late 1910s, small portable cameras were commonly available. Junior photographers may use a school darkroom to process film and print images or have one at home.

Talk, Intimidate, Persuade), Ride (or Drive Auto if age appropriate), any other one skill as personal or era specialty.

- **Badge:** starts with the **First Aid Badge** (page 31).

### Junior Sailor

The junior sailor dreams of life on the open water. Whether it’s the Navy or crewing a merchant steamer or commercial fishing vessel, this scout wants to be a part of the marine life. Even landlocked Westhaven has junior sailors. They may practice on local lakes and spend summertime on the ocean. Junior sailors have a strong command of knots, swimming, and lifesaving skills.

- **Obligations:** regular afterschool sessions, attendance at occasional events.
- **Trusted adult:** boat owners, fisherfolk, navy veterans.
- **Skills:** Electrical or Mechanical Repair, Fighting (Brawl), Navigate, Pilot (Boat), Survival, Swim, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Boating** , **Knot-Tying** , or **Weather Badge** (pages 30, 31 and 33).
- **Obligations:** helping with student yearbook or newspaper.
- **Trusted adult:** local newspaper photographer, local photography shop owner.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Photography), one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Psychology, Science (Chemistry), Stealth, Spot Hidden, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Photography Badge** (page 32).

### Junior Police Corps

Junior Police Corps members are training to attend the state police academy (or join the armed forces) upon graduation. Junior Police Corps members help direct traffic during parades and other community events. They have excellent leadership skills and, when they talk, people listen. Junior Police Corps members have positive relationships with local police, which could help gather information (or help them evade trouble!).

- **Obligations:** regular afterschool sessions, attendance at occasional events.
- **Trusted adult:** local police officers, small business owners.
- **Skills:** Fighting (Brawl), First Aid, Law, Psychology, Spot Hidden, one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast

### Library Helper

The library helper gleefully stays after school to help shelve books and decorate the school library. They may also volunteer at the town library or possibly even the archives at a local college or historical society. The library helper stays organized and pays attention to detail. All the time spent in the library makes them quite literate.

- **Obligations:** regular library shifts.
- **Trusted adult:** bookstore owners, local historical society, school and town librarian.
- **Skills:** Accounting, History, Language (Other), Language (Own), Library Use, Stealth, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Reading Badge** (page 33).

### Nature Enthusiast

The young nature enthusiast loves the outdoors. They might collect related items (bird eggs, dried flowers, seed pods, stones, etc.). They may carry a notebook to record bird sightings and other flora and fauna.

- **Obligations:** bird-watching club and nature club.
- **Trusted adult:** farmers, local librarian, and park keepers.
- **Skills:** Climb, Library Use, Listen, Natural World,

The scout-investigator assists in their religious community. This means regularly worshipping at their church, mosque, synagogue, temple, or other holy place.

- **Badge:** starts with the **Nature Badge** (page 32).

### Newspaper Carrier

These go-getters hustle to make a few coins each day, hopping on their bicycle early every morning with a sack of newspapers. They know the town’s neighborhoods thoroughly; this allows them to navigate shortcuts across alleys and vacant lots, saving time or allowing them to move about unnoticed. They have excellent relationships with many residents and business owners (at least, early risers!).

- **Obligations:** regular attendance at meetings, at least once per week.
- **Trusted adult:** local religious leader (priest, rabbi, etc.), members of their religious congregation.
- **Skills:** History, Language (Other), Library Use, Listen, one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Psychology, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Obligations:** making deliveries every morning.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Reading Badge** (page 33).


- **Trusted adult:** beat cops, drivers, hobos, local
- business owners.
- **Skills:** Climb, Ride (Bicycle), Sleight of Hand, Mechanical Repair, Natural World, Navigate, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Cycling Badge** (page 31).

### Shop Assistant

In the 1920s, people entered the workplace much younger; however, the game expects scout-investigators to remain in school, but perhaps have a part-time job. This may be assisting a family business or working in a local shop or café. Such work usually consists of menial tasks (washing dishes, stocking shelves, etc.).

**21** **CHAPTER 1**

- **Obligations:** regular shifts (after school or Saturdays mostly).
- **Trusted adult:** proprietor, regular customers.
- **Skills:** Accounting, two interpersonal skills (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade), Listen, Psychology, Spot Hidden, and two other skills as personal or era specialties.

- Badge: starts with the First Aid or Reading Badge (pages 31 and 33).

### Sports Prodigy

The athletic sports prodigy makes everything look easy. They hope someday to be a professional athlete or Olympic competitor. They already overshadow everyone in their chosen school or community sport. When the time comes, they will be awash in college scholarships to come throw, run, or swim.

- **Obligations:** training, practice, and games.
- **Trusted adult:** college recruiters, high school coaches, local business owners, local sports writers.


- **Skills:** Climb, Dodge, Fighting (Brawl), Jump, Swim, Throw, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Climbing** , **Cycling** , or **Swimming Badge** (pages 31, and 33).

### Street Punk

The street punk may know local criminals (maybe an older sibling or parent/other relative). As they get older, they likely engage with gangs and more significant crime—unless someone helps them change their ways. The street punk might occasionally work as a bagman: delivering illicit goods and returning payment in brown paper bags to their handlers. Some argue that street punks aren’t bad—just misunderstood.

- **Obligations:** running “errands,” following instructions.
- **Trusted adult:** petty criminals, local police (who may seek to redeem them).
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Acting) or Disguise, one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, or Intimidate), Fighting (Brawl), Locksmith or Mechanical Repair, Sleight of Hand, Spot Hidden, Stealth, any other one skill as personal or era specialty.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Cycling Badge** (page 31).

### Young Entrepreneur

The young entrepreneur’s creative, ordered mind brims with ideas for making money, and often has several schemes cooking at once. They may organize the school snack bar or have higher aspirations.

- **Obligations:** meeting customers or potential investors.
- **Trusted adult:** local businesspeople.
- **Skills:** Accounting, Law, Library Use, Listen, Persuade, Spot Hidden, any two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Reading Badge** (page 33).

### Young Scientist

Youngsters interested in the sciences likely belong to the school science club. They participate in fun and exciting science projects and learn about the scientific method. Club members may participate in student competitions to show off their science skills and inventions.

- **Obligations:** club meetings, occasional events.

_Sports Prodigy_ **22**

> — CREATING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

- **Trusted adult:** local academics and college professors, science teachers.
- **Skills:** any two science specializations, Library Use, one Language (Other), Language (Own), and three other skills as personal or era specialties.

- Badge: starts with the Nature or Radio Badge (pages 32 and 33).

### Upstate League

Most young people must find a career to support themselves and their family—but life is different for these spoiled rich kids, whose lifetime of wealth comes from just by being born into it. For these youth, the only acceptable career club is the networking provided by the Upstate League. Members go to exclusive camps in the summer, spend time doing volunteer work, fundraising, and internships. Almost all are scions of wealthy families.

However, some parents believe it’s important for their offspring to learn about the “commoners”—and one way to do this is joining the Wayfarer Scouts.

To take the Upstate League hobby, a scoutinvestigator’s **Family Credit Rating** (page 16) must be Rich.

- **Obligations:** extra classes, formal dinners, social functions.
- **Trusted adult:** other wealthy families, local political leaders, bankers, lawyers.
- **Skills:** Art/Craft (Any), Appraise, Language (Other), Language (Own), one interpersonal skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, Persuade), Ride (Horse), and two other skills as personal or era specialties.
- **Badge:** starts with the **Ability Badge** of your choice (page 30).

### Step Three: Decide Skills & Allocate Skill Points

Scout-investigators begin as Wanderers (aged 11–12) and have 200 points to spend among the eight skills listed for their chosen hobby. Scout-investigators begin with a limited skill set compared to their adult counterparts. While there are no “Personal Interest skills” (see _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook,_ page 36), each hobby allows players free choice over at least one skill (listed as a personal or era specialty). Additionally, scouts can earn **Ability Badges** (page 30) in the game, and many badges provide skill boosts.

- **Wanderer:** 200 starting skill points.
- **Rover:** 250 starting skill points.
- **Ranger:** 300 starting skill points.
- **Warden:** 350 starting skill points.

### Step Four: Create a Backstory

Most ideas and abilities that make a scout-investigator interesting and fun to play are a matter of choice—not dice roll results. Think about your scout-investigator and their family background, friends, enemies, and how they live. Perhaps the scout-investigator might be the relative of an adult investigator the player has developed and used in other scenarios!

Six categories populate the backstory section of the scout-investigator sheet—Personal Description, Home, Trusted Adult, Traits, Obligations, and Fears—try to write down at least one entry for each. It’s not essential to have an entry for every category, but the more you define, the more your scout-investigator comes to life. You can add further details or alter existing ones during play.

A scout-investigator’s backstory is a set of short, pithy statements that help guide roleplaying. It helps define your scout-investigator and reminds you how they relate to the world. Also, the Campfire Notes section below the backstory entries can be used to record other information, such as strange encounters, arcane tomes, and artifacts. Spells learned go here.

### Using Random Tables for Inspiration

Sometimes, creating background details on the fly can be difficult. The following lists and random tables provide a way to quickly determine background details—choose from the lists and roll or choose on the tables—or just use them for inspiration.

As appropriate, choose or roll 1D10 on each table. Each option has examples to get you started. Don’t feel constrained to use what you roll; if it doesn’t mesh with your character concept, roll again or choose differently. However, don’t dismiss it out of hand; something that at first seems incongruous may add an unexpected but welcome character twist.

Above all, these random tables serve as a springboard for your imagination. Each dice roll gives you something to embrace or reject; be sure to react and use it to fuel your imagination. Weave the background together to make a credible all-round character.

Skills increase through play (see **Scout-Investigator Development Phase** , page 47). If creating an older scout, begin with more skill points:

### Adult Skills

Adult skills can only be chosen by scoutinvestigators in exceptional circumstances, and then only with the Keeper’s permission. Some adult skill base values have been lowered to account for limited life experience and/or physical size.

### Personal Description

Think of a distinct look that sums up your investigator’s appearance (APP). Some possibilities follow (choose rather than roll): Bookish, Brawny, Dainty, Dirty, Dull, Elegant, Frail, Gawky, Hairy, Handsome, Mousy, Muscular, Ordinary, Pale, Plump, Pretty, Rosy, Scruffy, Sharp, Slim, Smart, Stout, Strapping, Stuffy, Sullen, Tanned, Ungainly, Untidy, Weary.

### Home

Consider your scout-investigator for a moment; write down a few ideas about their home and the people that share it with them. Usually, these are close family members, but it could be distant relatives, an adopted or foster family, or they may be in care. Note the occupation of the scout-investigators parents (or legal guardians).

Refer to **Family Credit Rating** (page 16) when describing the scout’s home and family. The scout’s Family Credit Rating dictates the affluence of the scout’s home. However, finances are not everything. While poverty can make life challenging, wealth does not automatically bring happiness.

A trusted adult treats the scout-investigator with respect and understanding. Select one adult contact per scout-investigator. This can be a contact listed under their chosen hobby, a relative, or other caring adult. Work with your Keeper to come up with a name, occupation, and place in the community. The Keeper could review named non-player characters (NPCs) in the setting or scenario and suggest an appropriate one (see **Trusted Adults in Westhaven** , nearby).

Trusted adults are NPCs controlled by the Keeper. When scout-investigators interact with their trusted adult, the adult should view the scout-investigator in a favorable light. This doesn’t mean that the adult believes everything the scout-investigators say, nor does it guarantee action. For further advice portraying trusted adults, see **Adults in Play** , below. The game effect of this relationship is story-based and does not confer any mechanical advantage.

As with any relationship, it can be soured and even broken. If the Keeper judges the trusted adult feels mistreated, exploited, or disappointed in the scout, they can state that the relationship soured. Record this on your scout-investigator sheet. A scout can get back into their trusted adult’s good books, either through contrition, an outstanding deed, or through the passing of time (see **Scout-Investigator Development Phase** , page 47).

### _**adults in Play**_

A divide exists between the world of children and the world of adults. Emphasizing this divide in _Campfire Tales_ helps differentiate it from regular _Call of Cthulhu,_ and reinforces what it means to be a scout-investigator. The youth and adults in this game are like strangers; their ways differ, and neither side understands the other.

This divide explains why scout-investigators struggle to have their concerns taken seriously by grown-ups. When scout-investigators warn the local deputy about monsters in the old mine, he just ruffles their hair and laughs. When they tell their parents, they are told to stop making up stories and do their chores.

Adults come to believe that their youthful years were different—somehow better—than those of the present day. Adults distrust youth and consider them unreliable and soft. Adults are ready to believe the worst about children, fundamentally believing they lack a sense of right and wrong (how this makes them different to adults is unclear).

Portray adult NPCs who are not trusted adults as wary of other people’s children: children must **25**


### Trusted Adults in Westhaven

Some possible candidates for trusted adults:

- **Dr. Deanna Bartlett** (page 57)—antiquities professor; excellent contact for someone with academic interests.
- **Nathan Booker** (page 58)—town council president; possible contact for someone from an influential family.
- **Andy Coolidge** (page 59)—police officer; potential contact for Junior Police Corps member.
- **Paul Glenn** (page 56)—scout leader; great contact for any scout.
- **Robert “Bob” Harley** (page 57)—candy store owner; possible contact for Shop Assistant.
- Jared Kulicky, _Westhaven Gazette_ reporter—excellent contact for Newspaper Carrier or Amateur Sleuth.
- **Walter Pennyforth** (page 61)—bookseller; superb contact for someone with academic or occult interests.
- Melissa Severson, the librarian at either at **H. H.**

**Bottoms Library** (page 64) or the school library— great contact for someone with academic interests.

- Rusty Smith (see **Smith Bros. Salvage** , page 64)— excellent contact for someone with criminal interests (Street Punk).
- **Starlite Diner Staff** (page 58): Sandy Turner, Leonard Abraham (the cook), or wait staff, Sheila or Maude Turner—great contacts for anyone interested in town gossip.
- Barbie Thingstad, actor at the **Palladium Theater** (page 58)—a superb contact for Junior Actors.
- Mrs. Abigail Wolfe or her sister Mildred run the **Westhaven Historical Society** (page 64)—both potential contacts for someone interested in local affairs.
- Margot Primrose or Geordie Benson, two of the old-timers who spend their time gossiping at **Bailey’s Top-Quality Hardware** (page 58)—excellent contacts for Amateur Sleuths or anyone interested in local history or town gossip.


### Creating Scout-investigators

be watched in case they steal or break something; if they tell stories of monsters they are humored or admonished for lying; if something is damaged in a public space, or litter or graffiti is found, children are to blame. In contrast, trust exists between the scoutinvestigator and their adult contact.

### Traits

A trait says something about your scout’s personality. Pick one of the following or roll 1D10. Take the result and make it specific and personal to your investigator:

1. Adventurous (e.g., loves a fresh challenge, always up for something new)

2. Charmer (e.g., always says the right thing, winning smile)

3. Dreamer (e.g., given to flights of fancy, highly creative)

4. Going places (e.g., ambitious, knows what they want to be when they grow up)

5. Hard worker (e.g., determined, never gives up)

6. Honest (e.g., painful to lie)

7. Kind (e.g., always helps a person in need, never speaks ill of anyone)

8. Loyal (e.g., stands by their friends, never breaks a promise)

9. Naïve (e.g., gullible, easily led)

10. Risk-taker (e.g., no concept of danger, thrill seeker)

### Fears

Each player should list at least one fear in their scoutinvestigator _’_ s backstory. Think of a fear as an image from a nightmare, never forgotten. The selected fear can begin as mild, but becomes more pronounced during play when a player fails a **Cool** roll (page 43). Pick one of the following or roll 1D10. Make the result specific and personal to your investigator:

1. Animals (pick one: dogs, rodents, snakes, etc.)

2. Creepy crawlies (cockroaches, flies, spiders)

3. Dark (guttering candles, unlit cellar, the woods at night)

4. Deprivation (running out of food, water, or other resources)

5. Ghosts (they could be watching right now, graveyards, undead)

6. Heights (bridges, ladders, trees)

7. Medical (blood, hospitals, needles)


> _Breakfast with Spiders_

### Chapter 1

8. Social interaction (embarrassment, public speaking, talking to strangers)

10. Water (the deep lake, the rapid stream, the rickety bridge)

### Obligations

Obligations add color to the scout-investigator’s lives and are both a burden and a boon. Adults appear to respect obligations, even though they often ignore their own. A scout-investigator mentioning obligations when talking with an adult can sometimes earn a bonus die on “social” skill rolls (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade). Also, prior obligations can be used to avoid new ones— _“Gee, Mr. Marshal, I’d like to help clean your drains, but I have rehearsal today.”_ Scout-investigators often team up—safety lies in numbers, after all. Sometimes, however, the Keeper may call upon an obligation to divide the scoutinvestigators— _“Say, didn’t you tell Mr. Marshal you have to stay late for rehearsal? Later that evening, it’s getting dark when you cycle home. That’s when you hear the howl, and something running along the road behind you!”_ Most **Hobbies** (page 17) have suggested obligations. Alternatively, they may have an obligation to someone else, such as doing chores, running errands, helping with the family business, and so on. Check your ideas with the Keeper, then add them under backstory for Obligations.

### Step Five: Gear & Possessions

The **Family Credit Rating** (page 16) section provides guidance on your players day-to-day living standards and what possessions they likely own. Also, some hobbies may imply an associated possession. For example, depending on Family Credit Rating, a musician may own an instrument or borrow one from their school. The Keeper should advise on any other starting equipment. Each player also starts play with a scout uniform and appropriate **Scout Badges** (nearby).

### Scout Badges

Part of being a scout is learning a variety of skills and knowledge. Scout badges mark their progress and achievement in their chosen areas. New badges are gained in the **Scout-Investigator Development Phase** (page 47). The two types are rank and ability badges:

- Rank badges: start at Wanderer (11–12 years old), and progress with age up to the rank of Warden (17–18 years old). Rank provides one or more special benefits once per scenario.

- **Ability badges:** provide a one-off skill increase and a special benefit once per scenario.

On the sheet, both rank and ability badges have two check boxes; one box notes the badge has been “Earned,” and one checks when its special benefit has been used in game. During a **Scout-Investigator Development Phase** (page 47), erase all “Used” checkmarks, indicating those special benefits are once more available.

### Starting Badges

All starting players begin play with the Wayfarer Scout Badge (page 29), the **Wanderer Badge** (page 29), and one **Ability Badge** (page 30) as specified by their hobby. If creating an older, experienced scout-investigator, choose a higher badge— **Rover**, **Ranger**, or **Warden** (nearby) and two or more (at the Keeper’s discretion) **Ability Badges** (page 30).

### Rank Badges

The five rank badges are earned as a scout-investigator gets older. When a rank badge is gained, the player should check the “Earned” box on their scoutinvestigator sheet.


### Creating Scout-investigators

### Wayfarer Scout Badge

All player possess this badge. Being a Wayfarer Scout is about fellowship and helping others. To reflect this, players can spend Luck points to:

- Improve a skill roll made by another squad member (standard _Call of Cthulhu_ players can only spend Luck points on their own skill rolls).
- A player can spend unlimited Luck points for this purpose; this ability can be used multiple times during a scenario.

### Rover Badge

13- and 14-year-old scout-investigators begin with this badge. The Rover badge replaces the Wanderer badge, and the game benefits of the Wanderer badge are no longer available to the player.

Use the Rover badge to refresh a player’s own Luck points. Once per scenario, the player may check their Rover badge, roll 2D6+6, and multiply the result by 5. They may use the new value to replace their scoutinvestigator _’_ s current Luck score.

### Ranger Badge

### Wanderer Badge

11- and 12-year-old scout-investigators begin with this badge. Quite useful, it automatically gains a successful outcome on any skill roll or opposed skill roll, _once per scenario_ . This can only be used on their own roll and cannot be used on behalf of another player. When the Keeper calls for a roll, regardless of difficulty, players may check their Wanderer badge to convert failure into success.

Note, this does not allow players to achieve the impossible: it only works when the Keeper allows a skill roll.

15- and 16-year-old scout-investigators begin with this badge. The Ranger badge replaces the Rover badge, and the game benefits of the Rover badge are no longer available to the player.

Use the Ranger badge to gain one bonus die on one of their own skill rolls, once per scenario. Before a skill roll is made, the player may take one bonus die.

- If the roll is successful, the player must check their scout-investigator _’_ s Ranger badge.
- If the roll is unsuccessful, the badge is not checked, and the player may take a bonus die on a future roll.

### Warden Badge

> _**Example:** the Keeper states that leaping the chasm requires a Jump roll and an Extreme success—the player fails the roll but decides to check their Wanderer badge to successfully land on the far side. A scout couldn’t leap the Grand Canyon regardless of their dice rolls, so checking their badge would not help._

17- and 18-year-old scout-investigators begin with this badge. The Warden badge replaces the Ranger badge, and the game benefits of the Ranger badge are no longer available to the player.


> _Wayfarer Scout Badge_

_Wanderer Badge_ _Ranger Badge_

Wardens spend much time helping others. Use the Warden badge to give one bonus die to someone else, once per scenario. Before a skill roll is made, the player may give another player one bonus die.

### _animal Friendship Badge_


- Hiking - Knot-Tying - Nature - Orienteering - Photography
- If the roll is successful, the player must check their Warden badge.
- If the roll is unsuccessful, the badge is not checked, and their player may give one bonus die on a future roll.

### Ability Badges

Twenty ability badges are available; collecting these badges is a central feature of the Wayfarer Scouts. Generally, one badge is awarded at the end of each scenario, and an additional one awarded during **ScoutInvestigator Development Phase** (page 47). When an ability badge is gained, check the “Earned” box by the badge on the scout-investigator sheet and record any skill increases or special powers.

Most ability badges grant a bonus die when making certain skill rolls. Be generous and flexible when players ask to use their badge.

- Animal Friendship

### Animal Friendship Badge

On receiving the animal friendship badge, increase Animal Handling by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for the scout-investigator to understand one simple message or concept that a dog (or similar, possibly a cat) is trying to communicate.

### Boating Badge

On receiving the boating badge, increase Pilot (Boat) by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Pilot (Boat) roll (or to another skill roll directly related to being around water, such as Swim).

- Boating
- Camping
- Climbing
- Crafting
- Cycling
- First Aid

### Camping Badge

On receiving the camping badge, increase Survival by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Mechanical Repair roll (or to another skill roll directly related to camping).

### Cycling Badge

On receiving the cycling badge, increase Ride (Bicycle) by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Ride (Bicycle) roll (or to another skill roll directly related to cycling, such as Mechanical Repair to mend a damaged bicycle).

### Climbing Badge

On receiving the climbing badge, increase Climb by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Climb roll (or to another skill roll directly related to climbing).

### Crafting Badge

On receiving the crafting badge, increase their choice of Art/Craft specialty by 10 points, for example, leatherwork, metalwork, pottery, woodwork, etc. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Art/Craft (any specialty) roll (or to another skill roll directly related to making and mending).

### First Aid Badge

On receiving the first aid badge, increase First Aid by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge to automatically grant a 4 -hit point recovery, with no skill roll required (the person’s hit points cannot go above maximum).

### Fishing Badge

On receiving the fishing badge, increase Survival by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, if in a suitable location and in possession of a little fishing tackle, check this badge to automatically catch enough fish for the squad’s dinner.

### Hiking Badge

On receiving the hiking badge, increase Survival by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one CON roll (or to another skill roll directly related to hiking).

### Orienteering Badge

On receiving the orienteering badge, increase Navigate by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Navigate roll (or to another skill roll directly related to the outdoors, such as Survival).

### Photography Badge

On receiving the knot-tying badge, the scout now uses knots more effectively than those without the badge.

On receiving the photography badge, increase Art/ Craft (Photography) by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Art/Craft (any specialty) roll. Or they may automatically succeed in taking one photograph of a specific subject, such as a person or monster (providing they have their camera). The photograph may be blurred or distant, open to criticism from skeptics, such as famous shots of UFOs or Bigfoot.

Usually, when an investigator ties a knot, the Keeper may allow a roll to determine the effectiveness of the knot. For example, if someone is tied up, the Keeper may allow the target a DEX roll to work their way out of the ropes over time. When a scout with the knot-tying badge ties a knot, it stays tied—it only comes free if someone purposefully unties the knot or cuts the rope.

During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one roll directly related to rope use and knots, such as Climb.

### Public Speaking Badge

On receiving the public speaking badge, increase a social skill (Charm, Fast Talk, Intimidate, or Persuade) by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one social skill roll. Alternatively, the Keeper may forgo the skill roll to allow an otherwise neutral adult to do as the scoutinvestigator requests.

### Nature Badge

On receiving the nature badge, increase Natural World by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Natural World roll (or to another skill roll directly related to nature and the outdoors, such as Survival).

### Radio Badge

On receiving the radio badge, increase Electrical Repair by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Electrical Repair roll (or to another skill roll directly related to radios—at a stretch, this could include Listen, as the scout is used to listening carefully).

### Swimming Badge

On receiving the swimming badge, increase Swim by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Swim roll (or to another skill roll directly related to swimming, such as Survival if the challenge relates to rivers or lakes).

### Weather Badge

### Reading Badge

On receiving the reading badge, increase two Language skills by 10 points each (for example, English and Spanish). During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Language (any) roll. Or they may automatically understand and translate one word or phrase in any non-Mythos language.

### Signals & Codes Badge

On receiving the signals and codes badge, increase Language (Signals) to 30% (or by 10 points, whichever is higher). During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Language (Signals) roll. Or they may automatically understand and translate one word or phrase in any non-Mythos language.

* _Language (Signals) is a new skill that includes Semaphore and Morse Code (see_ _**New Skills** , page 37)._

### Wilderness Survival Badge

On receiving the wilderness survival badge, increase Survival by 10 points. During play, once per scenario, check this badge for one bonus die on one Survival roll (or to another skill roll directly related to the outdoors).
