### The Scout Squad: an Investigator Organization

Investigator Organizations (see _Call of Cthulhu: Investigator Handbook_ , page 122) provide players with a framework on which to construct meaningful, long-term groups. In _Campfire Tales_ , the investigator organization is the scout squad, and the scoutinvestigators all belong. Players should decide on a name for their squad (e.g. Red Robins, Mad Wolves, Tin Lizzies, etc.), as well as their squad’s call—have fun with the players and encourage them to routinely use their squad’s call!

A squad consists of four to eight scouts. The group may wish to include NPC scouts in their squad, especially if you have fewer than four players. Use the **Ready-to-Play Scout-investigators** or create some from scratch. NPC squad members are usually played by the Keeper. However, with everyone’s agreement, a player may play one as a replacement or stand-in for their own investigator.

**Note: Assisted Fighting Maneuvers** (page 38) are most effective when performed by four individuals, hence the above suggestion.

Make it somewhere memorable, somewhere with character.

- At a squad member’s home, perhaps their basement, attic, garden shed, or treehouse.
- At an abandoned house or cabin; again, perhaps a cellar, attic, or maybe the whole property.
- At a den the squad built, such as a treehouse in the woods.

If the base is in someone’s home, household members may interfere in some way or overhear the squad’s discussions. If the base is outside the security of the family home, it becomes vulnerable in other ways; depending on location, other children or adults may discover it, and they could trash the place or invade.

### Resources

Over time, the squad collects resources. These can be mundane items, such as candles, maps, and snacks. Or they may be rarities. For example, **Boxcar Jim** (page 62) could present scout-investigators with trinkets and charms. These may be collected by the squad and stored either at their base or their homes.

### Optional Rules

Encourage discussion about the squad and decide issues, such as a **Squad Leader** (nearby), a **Base** (below), and **Resources** (nearby). These largely provide background color, but may become relevant in play.

### Squad Leader

The Wayfarer Scouts organization relies upon volunteers from the community for assistant scout leaders. They attend scout meetings, help with activities, hikes, and camps. One responsible adult is assigned to each squad and is known as their squad leader. A scout can go to their squad leader when in need; however, remember that the squad leaders are just regular people who volunteered and have their own family and work obligations.

### Base

Every squad has a base, a place to hang out. This can be negotiated between the Keeper and the players.

### Other Ways of Creating Scout-investigators

Different methods of scout-investigator creation exist, and different groups may agree on different methods. Some prefer a random approach; others prefer varying degrees of freedom to modify values, perhaps making characteristics fit a preconceived character concept or their perception of game balance.

For these reasons, alternative scout-investigator creation methods for creating are detailed here. Discuss with your group and decide which method or combination of methods best suits them.

### _**option 1: Placing Rolled Characteristics**_

Roll and record five rolls. Allocate the values as you wish among the five characteristics—CON, DEX, APP, INT, and POW. Do the same for the other three rolls for STR, SIZ, and EDU—as appropriate to the scout’s age (see **Step One: Generate Characteristics** , page 15). Allot the values as you wish among those three characteristics.


### Creating Scout-investigators

### _**option 2: Point-buy Characteristics**_

If you prefer to spend points rather than rolling dice, a total of 300 points can be spent among the five characteristics (CON, DEX, APP, INT, POW), each in the range of 35–85. The three remaining characteristics (STR, SIZ, EDU) depend on the age of the scout being created:

- Wanderer (11-12 years of age): spend 70 points among STR, SIZ, and EDU, each in the range of 10–45.

- Rover (13-14 years of age): spend 95 points among STR, SIZ, and EDU, each in the range of 15–50.

- Ranger (15-16 years of age): spend 135 points among STR, SIZ, and EDU, each in the range of 25–70.

- Warden (17-18 years of age): spend 165 points among STR, SIZ, and EDU, each in the range of 30–80.

- Rover: allocate 40, 50, 60, 70, 80 where you like among the five characteristics: CON, DEX, APP, INT, POW. For the remaining three characteristics (STR, SIZ, EDU), allocate 25, 30, 40 among them.

- Ranger: allocate 40, 50, 60, 70, 80 where you like among the five characteristics: CON, DEX, APP, INT, POW. For the remaining three characteristics (STR, SIZ, EDU), allocate 35, 45, 55 among them.

- Warden: allocate 40, 50, 50, 50, 60, 60, 70, 80 as you wish among the eight characteristics.

### Family Credit Rating: Alternative Method

For those who prefer to choose their scoutinvestigator’s **Family Credit Rating** (page 16), use this method. Choose the family credit rating and adjust the scout-investigators skill points accordingly:

- **Penniless:** gain an additional 20 skill points.

### _**option 3: Quick-fire Method**_

Use this method to get up and running quickly.

- **Poor:** gain an additional 10 skill points.
- **Average:** no change in skill points.
- **Wealthy:** reduce skill points by 20.

- Wanderer: allocate 40, 50, 60, 70, 80 where you like among the five characteristics: CON, DEX, APP, INT, POW. For the remaining three characteristics (STR, SIZ, EDU), allocate 20, 25, 25 among them.

- **Rich:** reduce skill points by 50.


### Chapter 2: Rules for Playing Scout-investigators

This chapter explores the game mechanics and options for playing with scoutT investigators. It covers new skills, a modified approach to combat, wounds and healing, character development, and introduces some altered and new rules.

### New Skills

Three new skills debut in _Campfire Tales—_ the Reassure skill is included here as it is not included in the _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook._ a troubled mind. This skill starts with a base value of APP divided by 5. Note that investigators cannot use this skill on themselves.

A successful Reassure roll enables the recipient to erase one checkmark (tick) from a **Distress Box** (page 43). If the investigator fails the roll, the distress box remains checked (ticked), while a fumbled or failed pushed roll checks (ticks) an additional distress box. Each scout-investigator can benefit from this only once per scenario or campaign chapter.

In addition, successful use helps a person suffering delusions to see through their hallucination.

- Family Credit Rating
- Language (Signals)

### Opposing Skill/difficulty Level:

- Reassure
- Ride (Bicycle)

### Family Credit Rating (random)

This skill replaces the Credit Rating skill used in _Call of Cthulhu_ —see **Chapter 1: Creating Scout-Investigators** , page 14, for details.

- **Regular difficulty:** help someone overcome mental trauma from a recent event; see through a delusion.
- **Hard difficulty:** help someone overcome mental trauma when the cause is still present, or the situation is otherwise desperate and dangerous.

### Language (signals) (01%)

This skill is a specialization of the Languages (Other). It covers Semaphore (visual signaling) and Morse Code (audio signaling), enabling communication over distances or where physical barriers may exist.

### Reassure (fifth App)

**Pushing examples:** taking more time; removing to a safer, calmer place.

**Sample consequences of failing a pushed roll:** target checks (ticks) one additional Distress Box; target reacts badly and the friendship between the characters is damaged, or the target lashes out at their helper.

Calm words and intuitive insight from a caring person can work wonders when it comes to soothing
### Ride (bicycle) (20%)

Anyone with this skill can ride a bicycle, make maneuvers, and cope with ordinary cycling problems. If the investigator wants to lose a pursuer or tail someone, a Ride (Bicycle) roll would be appropriate. Simply riding a bicycle does not, usually, require a skill roll.

### Assisted Fighting Maneuvers

To resolve an assisted fighting maneuver, two factors must be considered: fighting skill and Build values.

- First, players decide which scout-investigator takes the lead in the fighting maneuver—they use their Fighting skill for the attack roll.

### Opposing Skill/difficulty Level:

- **Regular difficulty:** weave through traffic at speed to avoid pursuit.
- **Hard difficulty:** travel off-road, weaving through trees and across uneven ground at speed to avoid pursuit.

Next, before the chosen scout-investigator (leader) compares Builds with their target, apply the following modifier:

- For each assisting ally, increase the lead scout’s Build by 1 point for this maneuver. So, if two scouts assist, the leader’s Build rises by 2.

**Pushing examples:** cycling as fast as possible, regardless of the risk; performing a daring maneuver.

**Sample consequences of failing a pushed roll:** buckling a wheel, crashing over the handlebars, running into someone.

### Combat

_Call of Cthulhu_ investigators face deadly perils, and death and injury can occur; however, for several reasons, combat is handled differently with scout-investigators:

- First, the tone of the game is intentionally different: think young adult fiction, and less brutal.
- Second, many scout-investigators have a negative Damage Bonus (DB). Attacks made by an unarmed person deal 1D3 damage: 2 points of damage being average. A person with Build –2 has their damage reduced by 2 points. Scout-investigators often begin with a DB of –2. This means they deliver zero damage on average—some players may feel frustrated by this.
- Third, these combat rules for scout-investigators are designed to promote teamwork: encouraging the players to work together in creative, enjoyable ways.

### Fighting Maneuvers

_Campfire Tales_ allows scout-investigators to lend a helping hand during maneuvers—something the regular rules do not allow.

The leader’s Build cannot be increased by more than 3 points. So, if the lead scout has Build – 2, their maximum Build is + 1 (negative 2 plus 3 equals 1). This means for assisted fighting maneuvers, four scouts is optimal.

**Note:** this method of increasing Build value is purposefully limited—otherwise, we might see a pile of 11-year-olds wrestling Great Cthulhu to the ground!

Next, compare the lead scout-investigator’s modified Build with their opponent’s to determine the number of penalty dice (see **Fighting Maneuvers** , nearby):

- If the lead scout-investigator’s modified Build is 3 or more points lower than their opponent’s, the maneuver fails.
- If the lead scout-investigator’s modified Build is 2 points lower than their opponent’s, apply two penalty dice.
- If the lead scout-investigator’s modified Build is 1 point lower than their opponent’s, apply one penalty die.
- If the lead scout-investigator’s modified Build equals their opponent, no modifiers are applied.
- If the lead scout-investigator has a modified Build greater than their opponent’s, the maneuver roll is made with one bonus die (no more than one bonus die is awarded by this method).


### Rules for Playing Scout-investigators

A scout-investigator cannot assist in a fighting maneuver if they have already taken their action for the present combat round. However, they can delay their action to assist an ally’s fighting maneuver on that ally’s turn in the combat round. So, everyone who assists in a combat maneuver must have held their previous action.

> _**Example** : three scout-investigators, Suzy, Ted, and Ayan have been caught taking an artifact from Colonel Grimm’s study. Mrs. Habersham is approaching as if to grab one of the scout-investigators. The scouts want to push Mrs. Habersham into the broom closet and wedge the door shut. The Keeper begins a combat round and establishes the DEX order: Suzy first (Fighting skill is used as the tiebreaker between her and Ayan, who have equal DEX), then Ayan, then Mrs. Habersham, and finally, Ted._

### _in Dex Order:_

- _Suzy: DEX 70, Fighting (Brawl) 40%, Build – 1._

- _Mrs. Habersham, the housekeeper: DEX 50, Fighting 25%, Build 0._

- _Ted: DEX 40, Fighting (Brawl) 25%, Build – 2._

> _Suzy could attempt the fighting maneuver alone; but her Build is one below that of Mrs. Habersham, so her Fighting roll would be made with a penalty die. She decides to hold her action so that she can assist either Ted or Ayan._

> _Ayan goes next. Now, the scouts face a dilemma. They would like to have Ted team up with them, but Mrs. Habersham acts before Ted. They decide to act on Ayan’s turn._

> _Suzy held her action, so she could lead the maneuver. Her Build increases by 1 (Ayan is helping) to 0, the same as Mrs. Habersham; so, she would roll her Fighting skill of 40% with zero penalty dice. If Ayan leads the maneuver, his Build increases by 1 (Suzy is helping) to + 1, 1 above Mrs. Habersham’s; so, he would roll his Fighting skill of 25% with one bonus die._

> _Which do they prefer? 40% with zero bonus die or 25% with one bonus die? It’s up to them, and they decide to go with Ayan leading the fighting maneuver._

- _Ayan: DEX 70, Fighting (Brawl) 25%, Build 0._


### Chapter 2

### Assisted Manuever Check List

1. What’s the maneuver’s goal?

2. Who takes the lead?

3. How many are assisting?

if appropriate for the story, a fighting maneuver may cause harm—clearly someone pushed down a well is going to suffer damage.

Refer to the Other Forms of Damage Table (see _Call of Cthulhu Starter Set_ , page 21, and _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook_ , page 124) for guidance on damage.

4. Compare lead’s modified build with opponent.

5. Determine lead’s bonus or penalty dice.

6. Roll for outcome.

> _Ayan rolls 48 and 18, taking the 18 because of the bonus die. The Keeper has Mrs. Habersham fight back, trying to grab Suzy (also a fighting maneuver). The Keeper rolls 38, a failure._

> _Ayan and Suzy’s fighting maneuver succeeds! Mrs. Habersham gets pushed into the broom closet and the door wedged shut as the scouts quickly get away!_

### Cumbersome Weapons

Many weapons are heavy, cumbersome objects. In an adult’s hands, this doesn’t pose a problem, but such items can challenge scout-investigators. Most weapons, except a small knife or light club, count as cumbersome for this purpose. Individual cases can be decided at the Keeper’s discretion.

When using a cumbersome weapon, like a big wood axe*, the scout-investigator must make a STR roll with each attack:

- **Success:** no penalty.

### Encouraging Assisted Fighting Maneuvers

By teaming up on fighting maneuvers, a group of scout-investigators should prove effective in combat. Encourage creativity in how they use fighting maneuvers, and remember to set clear goals when making them.

- **Failure:** apply a penalty die to their Fighting attack roll.
* _One appears outside the cabin in_ _**Tremors Below** (page 70)._

Examples goals for fighting maneuvers:

- Throw a bedsheet over the enemy to entangle them, giving the scouts time to make a getaway.
- Push their enemy down the stairs, down a well, or through a doorway—potentially inflicting harm.
- Run around their enemy with ropes, wrapping them until they are restrained.
- Jump on their enemy and pin them down.
- Attack with sticks as a group, not to inflict damage, but to frighten their enemy into running away.

All goals are negotiated between the players and the Keeper. For example, the final goal above, scaring an enemy away with sticks, might work on Rusty Smith (see **Smith Bros. Salvage** , page 64), or on a lone wolf; but it wouldn’t work against a shoggoth! The Keeper serves as final arbiter, but is encouraged to favor the players when it comes to setting goals.

**Note:** remember, the difference between a fighting maneuver and a regular fighting attack is the intent to do something rather than inflict harm. Of course,

> _Little Girl, Big Axe_


> — RULES FOR PLAYING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

If a scout-investigator prepares a firearm for one combat round, steadying the weapon (delaying their attack), make the attack roll without the STR roll. This way, they can take one shot every two combat rounds with no STR roll required.

**Note:** clearly, some teenagers are larger and stronger than some adults; ignore this rule if you wish, or apply it to all individuals (regardless of age) who have Build –1 or –2. However, this rule’s intent (for simplicity’s sake) is to reinforce the difference between playing scout-investigators and regular adult investigators.

that would incapacitate a standard _Call of Cthulhu_ investigator, and, sometimes, escape death itself!

### Faster Recovery

Wounded scout-investigators recover quicker: natural healing is increased to 2 hit points per day of rest. In addition, hit points granted for a successful First Aid or Medicine roll is increased to 1D4.

### Ignore Major Wounds

Do not use the major wounds rule for scout-investigators.

### Zero Hit Points

### Luck Spending

We recommend the optional Spending Luck rule (see _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook_ , page 99) when running _Campfire Tales_ . Besides calling for Luck rolls to determine the fickle hand of fate, players can also spend Luck to adjust dice rolls.

Often, this changes failure into success, but Luck can also be spent to alter the degree of success (from Regular to Hard, or from Hard to Extreme). To improve a skill roll, the player “burns” Luck points, spending the amount necessary to reverse a failure (or increase the level of success). Luck points cannot be spent on Luck or Cool rolls.

For example, if you have Climb 50% but rolled 55 (a failure), you could spend 5 Luck to adjust the roll to 50 (a success). The only limit on Luck spending is the “current Luck” held by the scout-investigator; be aware that Luck rolls made later in the game use the new lower value!

Scout-investigators regain spent Luck points at the start of a new scenario by rolling against their current Luck value: if they fail the roll, they gain +1D10 points. Otherwise, if the roll is equal to or less than the scout-investigator’s current Luck score, no points are recovered.

At zero hit points, a scout-investigator automatically falls unconscious. Record the Current Hit Points as “0,” rather than using negative values. The only exception is when a scout-investigator suffers a single wound that deals equal to or greater than their maximum hit point total (see **Dying** , nearby).

In certain circumstances, such as a monster consuming or completely ripping the scout-investigator apart, the Keeper may rule death seems inevitable— the scout-investigator’s only option is **Cheating Death** (page 42).

### Dying

A scout-investigator who suffers a single wound equal to or greater than their maximum hit point total is “Dying” and automatically falls unconscious.  Record the Current Hit Points as “D” for dying rather than a number. To return to play, a “Dying” character must receive treatment—a successful First Aid or Medicine roll:

- **Successful First Aid roll:** the patient gains 1D4 hit points (an Extreme success automatically grants 4 HP recovery). Remove the “D” for “dying,” and enter the number of hit points recovered as their current hit point score.

### Wounds & Healing

Scout-investigators generally have fewer hit points than their adult counterparts. To make them more durable, _Campfire Tales_ adopts some _Pulp Cthulhu_ rules; this allows scout-investigators to shake off wounds **41**


- **Following a successful First Aid roll:** a follow-up Medicine roll may be made later, granting an additional 1D4 hit points (an Extreme success granting 4 hit points recovery).
- First Aid and Medicine rolls cannot increase a patient’s hit points beyond their maximum hit point total.

Once brought back into play, the scout-investigator’s natural healing continues at the rate of 2 hit points per day of rest until they return to their maximum hit point total.

### Wounds & Healing Checklist

**Healing:** 2 HP per day. **First Aid/Medicine:** 1D4 HP. **Major Wounds:** ignore. **Zero HP:** unconscious. **Dying:** single wound equal/greater than max HP. **Cheating Death:** spend all Luck (min. 30 points).

A “Dying” scout-investigator has the option of **Cheating Death** (nearby)—provided they possess sufficient Luck points.

### Cheating Death

If a scout-investigator possesses at least 30 Luck points, they can cheat death. Scout-investigators must spend _all_ their Luck (30 points minimum) to avoid death.

In such situations, the scout-investigator’s Luck value is reduced to zero; their condition slightly improves, and they get to live. The character immediately regains 1D6+1 hit points (not exceeding their maximum hit point total). The player must explain how their scout-investigator escapes the worst of their injuries and survives.

### Death

If a “Dying” scout-investigator does not receive successful medical attention within the hour, and lacks the Luck points for **Cheating Death** (nearby), the character dies.


> — RULES FOR PLAYING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

### Cool

Sanity plays a central role in _Call of Cthulhu,_ but _Campfire Tales_ replaces it with Cool. When an adult investigator would make a Sanity roll, a scoutinvestigator makes a Cool roll instead. Do they “keep cool?” Or do they “lose their cool?”

A scout-investigator’s Cool value is equal to their POW (it does not rise and fall as Sanity does). When making a Cool roll, a roll equal to or below their Cool value succeeds. A success indicates that the scout-investigator took the event in stride and avoided distress. Failing a Cool roll always evokes a response. The nature of this response can vary greatly and depends on the situation. How scout-investigators respond to failing a Cool roll is up to the player.

The “five Fs” below provide excellent starting points. However, allow players to come up with something else if they wish.

### Cool Checklist

Replaces Sanity.

**If failed** , player chooses one:

- Fawn
- Fight
- Flight
- Flop
- Freeze

And checks one Distress box (Stressed, Jumpy, Upset).

- (Optional: involuntary action.)

**Recovery:** erase one Distress check if:

- Spends night safe at home.
- **Fawn:** if you’re nice to it, maybe it will be nice to you.
- **Fight:** lash out—this may be physical (punches and kicks), or verbal (yelling and harsh words).
- Spends time with squad telling stories.

Erase all three Distress checks during the ScoutInvestigator Development Phase.

- **Flight:** run for your life!
- **Flop:** your knees buckle and you collapse.
- **Freeze:** don’t move, perhaps hoping it won’t see you; stand still and scream loudly!

Depending on story events, when a Cool roll is failed, the Keeper can—optionally—dictate one brief involuntary action for the scout-investigator. For example:

> _**Example:** Holly the scout-investigator sees a ghoul in the basement, and the Keeper asks for a Cool roll. Holly fails the roll and chooses flight. As she flees through the kitchen, the Keeper chooses to have Holly knock a pot to the floor—as an involuntary action. The resultant clatter awakens the household._

### Distress Boxes

- **Bump into something or someone:** crashes into an oil lamp, vase, fellow scout-investigator, etc.
- **Drop something:** drops something they were holding (flashlight, glasses, key, etc.).
- **Make noise:** makes a hullabaloo, perhaps saying something inappropriate, a scream, etc.

**Note:** the involuntary action should be only last a moment. The Keeper does not control the scoutinvestigator for any longer than that. Also, note that that involuntary actions are not mandatory every time someone fails a Cool roll.

Three distress boxes appear on the scout-investigator sheet, labelled “Stressed,”  “Jumpy,”  and “Upset.” Every time a scout-investigator fails a Cool roll, the player must choose and check one box on their investigator sheet—so, the player chooses whether their scout is scared, jumpy, or upset.

- When one or two boxes are checked, the scoutinvestigator is becoming distressed—at present, this is purely a roleplaying effect (no additional mechanical effect exists at this point).
- When all 3 boxes are checked, the scout-investigator is “distressed.” The player should roleplay this as


### Chapter 2

they see fit. In addition, the scout-investigator becomes subject to Delusions (see _Call of Cthulhu Starter Set: Book 2_ , page 16; or _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook_ , page 162).

### Distress Recovery

A player may erase one checkmark from a distress box (i.e., “Stressed,” “Jumpy,” or “Upset”) when any of the following occurs:

### Cool & Fear

When making a Cool roll, if the scout-investigator’s **Fear** (page 27) plays a significant role in the moment, apply one penalty die.

When a scout-investigator fails a Cool roll, play up the fear noted in their backstory, whether it’s present in the scene or not. Always go with the story: if featuring a particular fear feels out of place, don’t force it—it doesn’t have to be snakes every time!

- Spends a night at home, safely tucked up in bed. Note: this does not count if their home is no longer a safe place (as is the case in **Hands of Winter** , page 150).
- Spends the evening with squad members sitting around a campfire telling tales, with food and a comfortable place to sleep. Note: this does not work if the other squad members have all their distress boxes checked.

- All 3 boxes automatically clear during the **ScoutInvestigator Development Phase** (page 47).

### Adversity

Wayfarer Scouts embrace the challenges of hiking and camping in the great outdoors. But just what are these challenges, and how can the Keeper bring them into play?

Adversity in _Campfire Tales_ comes in five forms: cold, hunger, lost, overburdened, and sore. Each form of adversity has an associated dice roll or effect. Ask scouts who succeed in their dice roll about how they overcome the adversity; should they fail, discuss all adverse effects they experience. Either way, the adversity should feed into the story.

_Tired Feet_ **44**

> — RULES FOR PLAYING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

As Keeper, use these rules to remind the players of the challenges they face, but avoid overuse—otherwise, players may feel they are being punished.

Scout-investigator sheets feature a checkbox for each of the five forms of adversity. Players should check a box when their scout-investigator fails the roll associated with that form of adversity; erase the checkmark when they are restored (see the various forms of **Restoration** , following).

- If a scout-investigator has 3 adversities checked, Cool rolls are made with one penalty die.
- If a scout-investigator has all 5 adversities checked, Cool rolls are made with two penalty dice.

### Cold

Keeping warm can be a constant battle in the winter, especially outdoors. Appropriate clothing and hot food can do a lot to warm a person. Moving around helps too—a person soon gets cold if stationary, especially if sitting or lying on the ground. Without food, the body lacks fuel to generate body heat. Exposure to cold water or wet clothes saps body heat rapidly.

When cold is constant, the Keeper should call for just enough “Adversity: Feeling Cold” rolls to remind players of their environment.

### _**adversity: Feeling Cold**_

When scout squads spend prolonged periods in the cold without proper gear, the Keeper can ask for a **Survival** roll. Use the scout with the highest skill. Optionally, apply one penalty die in freezing weather.

- **Success:** the scouts find a way to keep warm; ask the players how they have managed to keep warm: what shelter have they made; what have they found to eat; have they managed to make a fire?
- **Failure:** the scouts’ teeth begin to chatter, and the cold gnaws at their bones. No one recovers any hit points (see **Wounds & Healing** , page 41) until they warm up. Also, if the weather is freezing, each scoutinvestigator loses 1 hit point, and makes a CON roll—if failed, they develop a nasty cold.

### _**restoration**_

Donning warm, dry clothing; eating hot food; sitting by a fire for 30 minutes.

### Hunger

The body burns many calories when hiking, and a hearty meal does a lot to raise morale. Even one missed meal can cause frayed tempers. Food also helps the body generate heat, and in low temperatures, people need to eat to stay warm.

### _**adversity: Feeling Hungry**_

After a scout squad misses a couple of meals, the Keeper can ask for a **Survival** roll. Use the scout with the highest skill. Apply one penalty die if the land is barren.

- **Success:** the scouts find food; ask the players what they have found (add some description—did it need preparation?)
- **Failure:** the scouts’ bellies rumble. After a day of this, ask for a **Cool** roll—if failed, tempers fray. Also, no one recovers any hit points (see **Wounds & Healing** , page 41) until they eat.

### _**restoration**_

Eat something. A hearty cooked meal is best, but anything tastes delicious when you are hungry.

### Lost

Wayfarer Scouts learn orienteering: navigating using maps and a compass. Long hikes are hard work; going the wrong way or finding you’ve gone in a circle and ended up where you began demoralizes you. Picture yourself, lost in the wilderness; it’s starting to rain, and the sun quickly sets behind the trees—getting lost can be soul-destroying.

### _**adversity: Feeling Lost**_

When a scout becomes lost in the wilderness, after failing a Navigate roll, the Keeper can ask for a **Cool** roll. Apply one penalty die if a scout is alone.

- **Success:** the scout keeps their morale up; ask them how—perhaps they sing a cheery walking song?
- **Failure:** the scout loses their cool; the player must describe how—perhaps they become upset, break something, or head off alone. Do they split up or make enough noise to be heard by something or someone?


### Chapter 2

### _**restoration**_

Finding a familiar location, perhaps a road or the edge of town, and knowing that they are no longer lost can put a smile back on the scouts’ faces. Of course, a person can be lost in a city, but the same applies. Running across a friendly person who knows where they are can also dispel the sense of being lost.

### Overburdened

When hiking, Wayfarer Scouts carry what they need in a backpack: a sleeping bag, camping equipment, food, dry clothes, etc. The pack may not feel too heavy when starting, but it feels much heavier after hiking a few miles. Supporting or carrying a friend can be an additional burden. Running away while overburdened is near impossible.

- If the scout-investigator carries a full pack (including tent and sleeping bag): reduce MOV by 3.
- The Keeper should adjudicate other burdens, using the above as a guide.

### _**restoration**_

If a scout-investigator needs to run, their only option may be to dump their gear. Of course, this means that later, they don’t have needed items such as dry clothes or food. Jettisoning a pack only takes a moment, and the action does not slow the scout-investigator down.

### Sore

A blister, a splinter, something lodged in the eye, a skinned knee, an insect bite—none of these are lifethreatening or cause damage, but any one can niggle away at a person, leaving them distracted and irritable.

### _**adversity: Overburdened**_

A scout-investigator can always trudge along with their pack. It’s when they hurry that it becomes an issue. When the scout-investigator runs (such as when in a chase), adjust their movement speed as follows:

- If the scout-investigator carries a day pack (without tent and sleeping bag): reduce MOV by 1.

### Adversity Checklist

**Cold:** Survival roll. **Hunger:** Survival roll. **Lost:** Cool roll. **Overburdened:** modify MOV rate (–1 or –3). **Sore:** CON roll. **Three checked:** one penalty die on Cool rolls. **All five checked:** two penalty dice on Cool rolls.


### _**adversity: Feeling Sore**_

Sometimes scouts push themselves too hard, such as long hike with insufficient rest stops, or hours at a hard physical task unfamiliar to them. When a scoutinvestigator overexerts themselves, ask for a **CON** roll:

- **Success:** the scout is tired, but otherwise, fine.
- **Failure:** the scout feels sore somehow; discuss the specifics with the player—does the scoutinvestigator have blisters, for example. This outcome feeds into the roleplaying, rather than any game penalty—significant injury is gauged in hit points, but items like splinters and blisters don’t merit a damage roll. If the Keeper feels it’s appropriate to the story, apply one penalty die to any subsequent Cool roll for that scout-investigator.

“Feeling Sore” rolls could be appropriate in other situations, such as:

- Forcing one’s way through thick undergrowth may require a STR roll. Afterward, a CON roll might determine if they are sore from scratches and cuts.
- If a DEX roll is failed while wading a stream, one might slip and fall. Afterward, a CON roll might determine to see if they are sore from bruising.
- Next to lakes or swamps in warm conditions, Keepers might call for a Luck roll to see if scouts get bitten by insects. Afterward, a CON roll might determine if they are sore with itchy bites.


> — RULES FOR PLAYING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

- If the scout has slept outside at night without their camping gear, a CON roll might determine if they are sore from lying on tree roots or hard ground.

Young people may change interests as they age, and at each new rank a scout-investigator may choose a different hobby. Spend any additional skill points among those listed under the new hobby.

### _**restoration**_

Some care and attention, along with salve and a bandage, can ease the discomfort. The Keeper could call for a First Aid roll, with success easing the scoutinvestigator’s discomfort. If no first aid kit is available, consider raising the difficulty to Hard.

### Leap of Logic

The scout-investigators begin as naïve 11-year-olds. But in TV and films, the kids sometimes connect the dots in ways that leave viewers wondering, “ _How on Earth did they figure that out?”_ The game refers to this as a “Leap of Logic.” When the Keeper calls for a Leap of Logic roll, all scout-investigators should make an INT roll.

- A Leap of Logic allows Keepers to tell players facts they might not otherwise know or figure out (such as a clue).
- The scout-investigator with best level of success makes the leap of logic. In case of a draw, the highest INT value wins.
- If everyone fails the INT roll, the Keeper decides the outcome. They may still wish to give the investigator with the highest INT a hint—that scout-investigator should now be feeling, _“Wait, I had it for a moment—let me think…,”_ with the Keeper feeding hints as they see fit.

### Scout-investigator Development Phase

A scout-investigator begins at the age of 11 or 12 as a Wanderer. After one or more sessions, the Keeper can announce that time has passed, and the scout progresses to Rover; they are now 13–14 years old. If this were a TV show, it would be a following season; both actor and character would have aged a year or two.

The scouts grow, both physically and mentally, and three characteristics (Strength, Size, and Education) may alter at each step. The scouts are also learning rapidly; this is reflected in additional skill points available to them.

### Education & Own Language Skill

The **“** Own Language” skill begins equal to the scout’s EDU characteristic, which can increase during the scout-investigator development phase. Be sure to add all increases in the EDU characteristic to the scout’s Own Language skill.

> _**Example: Bobby’s Own Language (English) starts equal to EDU 22. During character creation, he spends 8 skill points increasing Own Language to 30. When progressing from__**Wanderer to Rover** (nearby), his EDU increases by 5 points to 27. So, he raises his Own Language (English) skill by 5 points to 35._

### Progressing: Wanderer to Rover

Roll for the following changes when moving from Wanderer to Rover (becoming 13–14 years old):

- Strength (STR): add 5 to the scout’s present STR value. Then, roll 2D6+ 3, and multiply the result by 3. If the product exceeds the scout’s STR, use the new value.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 5 and multiply the result by 3. If the product exceeds the scout’s SIZ, use the new value.

- Education (EDU): add 5 to the scout’s present EDU value. Then, roll 2D6+ 3, and multiply the result by 3. If the product exceeds the scout’s EDU, use the new value.

**Note:** applying the changes above guarantees a 5-point minimum increase in Strength and Education. However, no similar Size increase guarantee exists. Be sure to check for any changes in hit points, Build, damage bonus, and Own Language skill after increasing characteristics.

- The scout-investigator may choose to continue with the same hobby or start a new one. A scout can have only one hobby at a time.

- Strength (STR): add 5 to the scout’s present STR value. Then, roll 2D6+ 4, and multiply the result by 4. If the product exceeds the scout’s STR, replace it with this new value.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 5 and multiply the result by 4. If the product exceeds the scout’s SIZ, replace it with this new value.

### Coming of Age: Becoming an Adult Investigator

Roll for the following changes when progressing to an adult investigator:

- Strength (STR): roll 2D6+ 5 and multiply the result by 5. If the product exceeds the scout’s STR, use the new value.

- Size (SIZ): roll 2D6+ 5 and multiply the result by 5. If the product exceeds the scout’s SIZ, use the new value.

- Education (EDU): roll 2D6+ 5 and multiply the result by 5. If the product exceeds the scout’s EDU, use the new value.

**Note:** the above does not guarantee any increases. Be sure to check for any changes in hit points, Build, damage bonus, and Own Language skill after increasing characteristics. The investigator is no longer a scout-investigator. The skill points that have been allocated during their time as a scout remain, and may be added to as follows:

- Refer to the _Call of Cthulhu Starter Set_ , _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook_ , or the _Call of Cthulhu: Investigator Handbook_ and choose an occupation.
- Calculate your occupational skill points using the characteristics specified alongside the occupation (as if you were creating a new investigator). Add to this skill point total a number of points equal to your investigator’s INT × 2 (these are referred to as Personal Interest Points). This is your investigator’s new skill point total.

**49** **CHAPTER 2**

- As a Warden, your investigator has already spent 350 skill points. Subtract 350 from the skill point total you calculated in the previous paragraph. This is the number of points you now must spend on your occupational skills. As everyone should have points to spend at this stage, this number cannot be below 100.

- Skill point increases derived from scout badges remain. However, all other scout badge effects cease. All those badges are now just pieces of cloth. Perhaps someday, your investigator may gaze nostalgically at their faded badges and recall happy adventures as a Wayfarer Scout.
- Develop your investigator’s backstory, building upon your scout-investigator’s history and experience so far—refer to the _Call of Cthulhu Starter Set_ , _Call of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook_ , or _Call of Cthulhu: Investigator Handbook_ , and see the ideas in the box, nearby.

### A Note About Using Adults in These Stories

While most gamers are accustomed to playing adult characters, this book focuses on youth characters as protagonists. The adults are, by design, pushed to the periphery of the story. It may be tempting to introduce a playable scout leader or other adult into the mix. However, this focuses on one investigator as the _de facto_ leader. This can lead to an uneven style of play where the other players feel marginalized. The intent of this material is for scout-investigators to all be peers and work as a team to achieve their goals.

For that reason, we recommend restricting adults to use as non-player characters (NPCs) in these stories. This may include parents, scout leaders, police, and town residents. The focus of this game is the scouts: those young people, out camping in the wilderness, telling their _Campfire Tales_ .


**50** _Family Time_

> — RULES FOR PLAYING SCOUT-INVESTIGATORS

### Alternative Characteristic Rolls

Note that the dice rolls for adult investigators’ characteristics provided in this chapter differ from the ones provided in regular _Call of Cthulhu._

- The rolls presented here are 2D6+ 5 multiplied by 5, giving characteristics a range of 35 to 85, with an average of 60.

- The rolls in regular _Call of Cthulhu_ are different: five of the characteristics (STR, CON, DEX, APP, and POW) use 3D6 multiplied 5, giving a range of 15 to 90, with an average of 52.5; the other three (SIZ, INT, and EDU) use 2D6+6 multiplied 5, giving a range 40 to 90, with an average of 65. The average over the eight characteristics using that method is just over 57, so the difference between the two methods is minimal.

Those who wish to avoid the lower end (30 and lower) may prefer to use the rolls provided here. This method uses the same rolls for all eight characteristics, which makes the process a little more straightforward.

### Downtime Activity

The scout-investigators are assumed to live in Westhaven, and life goes on between scenarios. Depending on how the story unfolds around your gaming table, some loose ends or unresolved issues may remain after completing a scenario.

Focusing on characters and player-led investigation is strongly encouraged and can help make a game come alive. The Keeper may wish to incentivize participation in downtime activity by rewarding scout-investigators with an additional **Ability Badge** (page 30). Some topics that downtime activity might address include the following:

- Activities centered around the scout-investigator’s **Obligations** (page 28) and the people associated with them.
- Following up on previous scenarios: at the library, bookshops, or with other townspeople.
- Home life: involving other family members.
- Life around town: involving other kids and regular townsfolk.
- School life: involving teachers or other pupils.
- Scouting life: fundraising (litter picking, sponsored events, etc.), camping, hiking, etc.

A popular method with some groups is to focus a short scene on one scout-investigator at a time. This allows their fellow players to roleplay other characters in the scene. Some players enjoy the chance to play someone else’s mother or brother, or fellow school children. This can provide a lot of humor, but can also be played straight. There should be little need for the use of character sheets or dice rolls. Alternatively, rather than roleplaying, a player may prefer to describe a scene involving their scout-investigator and something they did between adventures.

Whichever approach you use, create a rich story and shine a spotlight on the scout-investigators’ lives.


### Chapter 3: Westhaven

hese scenarios take place in the fictional town of Westhaven, located 40 miles (64 T km) west of Arkham, Massachusetts. This idyllic town sits near the New Hampshire border and abuts great forests that stretch north for miles. As you head north out of Westhaven, the increasingly larger hills turn into small mountains, the White Mountains of New Hampshire.

The Atlantic Ocean seacoasts can be reached quickly by turning east and traveling through Massachusetts, New Hampshire, Rhode Island, or Maine. Many working fishing villages dot the coastline, bringing in whitefish from the cold Atlantic or delicious lobsters and clams from the coast.

### History of Westhaven

Westhaven was founded in 1689 by a breakaway Puritan sect who disagreed on the source of divine providence. Rumors swirled among the locals that the sect’s true motivations were more sinister. Some accused the Westhaven group of collaborating with dark forces. As a result, Westhaven became isolated and shunned by neighboring towns for decades. Despite such isolation, or perhaps because of it, many tragic events occurred during the following years.

The Salem Witch Trials reached their zenith in the late 17[th] century, gripping the region in terror; many residents feared for their lives. Unfortunately, Westhaven also gave in to this madness. Samuel Minter, a theologian, arrived in 1693 and convinced local elders he was drawn by, “ _a sickening smell of witchcraft permeating the area._ ”

Minter studied with Cotton Mather, and he put on a showman-like display that captivated locals. His fiery public sermons and tales of hunting the “ _devil’s brides_ ” enthralled and terrified townsfolk. During the first scenario, **Tremors Below** (page 70), scoutinvestigators may find a tome penned by Minter.

### Westhaven in 1918

Westhaven’s population is 3, 270, making it larger than many neighbors, some of which only have a few hundred residents. The town’s elected officials pass local ordinances and maintain the town operations and finances. The town council is presided over by a president elected by the council from within their ranks.

The current town president, Nathan Booker (page 58), owns a local business and is a family man. While genial, he is not a particularly strong leader and often defers to council vice-president, **Thomas Cline** (page 60). Cline maintains a vast network of contacts and uses those connections to his advantage. He also secretly belongs to the **Sons of Seth** (page 60) and features prominently in the final scenario in this book, **Shadow Over Westhaven** (page 132).

Westhaven is vibrant by most standards. The Spring Festival celebrates the renewal of the Earth; however, its odd way of celebrating the “death of winter” carries strange, somber tones. No one knows why, it has always been that way.

The Independence Day celebration is especially festive, complete with loud fireworks to end the day. Children decorate their bikes with streamers and parade down Main Street to the delight of parents and local shop owners. Every fall, the harvest parade wends through town, complete with horse- and ox-drawn floats.

In summer, children swim in ponds and rivers, and many play junior league baseball. Last year, **53**

### Notable Locations & Npcs

Featured locations are detailed in this section. While key NPC (non-player character) profiles are given within the location where they most often appear, others are left for Keepers to create if needed.

### 1. Scout Hut

This wooden hut is Unit #104: the Westhaven Wayfarer Scout’s home base. Here, various squads regularly meet to play games, work toward badges, and plan hikes and trips.

### **notable Npc: Paul Glenn** (see Above).

### 2. Middlewich College

One reason for the town’s relative prosperity is Middlewich College. This small, liberal arts private school was founded in 1822 by Herbert H. Bottoms. Bottoms was a local business leader who moved from Ireland as a young man and prospered in timber. He was keen on education due to his mother being a schoolteacher back in Derry.

Bottoms also donated money for the town library, which bears his name. While many people attend Middlewich College seeking teaching or business degrees, it has also become well-known for its Antiquities and Archeology Department.

### **notable Npc: Dr. Deanna Bartlett** (opposite).

### 3. Harley’s Sweet Shoppe

The ice cream parlor (or soda fountain) is one of the most iconic meeting spots of the early 20[th] century. Harley’s is a quintessential example of this type of establishment and a social anchor of the community. The shop’s owner, Bob Harley, is outgoing and friendly. Bob makes ice cream and a wide variety of confections from scratch; however, his cream sodas are by far his customers’ favorite. The vanilla-pistachio ice cream soda is his best-seller.

The interior features a 30 foot (9 m) long service counter with round stools fixed to the floor. A soda fountain and ice cream coolers sit behind the counter. Black- and white-checkered tile adorns the floor. Bright polished chrome and decorative light sconces festoon the walls.

**Notable NPC: Robert “Bob” Harley** (opposite).


### Westhaven

### **dr. Deanna Bartlett,** _**age 38**_ **,** _**antiquities Professor**_

Doctor Bartlett lectures at Middlewich College and is a favorite among her students. She specializes in ancient civilizations; she built the Antiquities and Archeology Department from scratch when she landed her first teaching position here out of college. She had researched ancient cultures at several universities around the world, ultimately earning her doctorate from Miskatonic University.

- **Traits:** always has a book in her hand and glasses perched on her nose.

### Chapter 3

### 4. Westhaven Central Park

In the center of Westhaven, just off Main Street, lies a five-acre park created in the town’s early days as a place to share garden plots. Later, it transitioned into a recreational space with open fields and wooded areas. This popular destination for families sees lots of traffic on weekends and holidays. The park also houses the town baseball field that the junior league uses for its games.

### 5. Palladium Theater

At night or on Saturday afternoons (especially when it’s raining), locals flock here to see the latest releases from Hollywood. Hitting the theater recently are silent classics like as _Cleopatra_ (starring Theda Bara), _Easy Street_ (starring Charlie Chaplin), and _The Butcher Boy_ (featuring Fatty Arbuckle and Buster Keaton).

### 6. Bailey’s Top-quality Hardware

One of the many Top-Quality Hardware franchises, Bailey’s has been a Westhaven staple for decades. Started in 1887 by Duncan Bailey, the firm was passed down to his son, Ted; now, his grandson, Bertram runs the place. Everyone calls him “Bert.” Like many hardware stores of the day, it functions as a department store for small towns; this allows rural residents to purchase goods usually only available in urban areas.

Scout-investigators likely visit Bailey’s regularly. They sell fishing tackle and bait, toys and candy, the latest adventure magazine, and baseball mitts. The scouts know Bert and many old-timers that tarry to tell hunting, fishing, and war stories. This makes Bailey’s a tremendous resource of local information that scoutinvestigators can tap when needed.

### 7. Starlite Diner

The Starlite is known for its homemade pies and country cooking. A breakfast of eggs, fried potatoes, bacon, and toast usually starts the day. Their lunch special sandwich is fried liver and onions with slaw. The dinner menu boasts turkey on toast with gravy, which is also their best seller. Country apple with hard cheese is their most popular pie, but the rhubarb, when in season, is also a crowd pleaser.

A marble lunch counter with stools for customers runs nearly the length of the interior. Foot-square light green tiles cover the floor, and oversized brass light fixtures grace the walls. The front windows have large decorative booths where patrons can sit while they eat and chat.

Behind the main lunch counter, Sandy Turner slings coffee. Behind her, steam from the grill can be seen through a large window into the kitchen area. Back in the kitchen, Leonard Abraham does most cooking. On busy days, several wait staff work the room, including Sheila and Maude Turner.

### Westhaven Police

The police work out of **Westhaven Town Hall** (below); they maintain a cordial relationship with both council president **Nathan Booker** (opposite) and vice president **Thomas Cline** (page 60). The town employs several officers, but for continuity, **Andy Coolidge** (nearby) usually features in play. Some volunteers (young and old) lend their assistance and support to the police, and the town boasts an active **Junior Police Corps** (page 20), of which a scout-investigator may be a member.

### **andy Coolidge,** _**age 39**_ **,** _**police Officer**_

Coolidge has worked at the Westhaven Police Department since he was a teen. The son of freed enslaved people, his family moved north from Virginia for a chance at a better life in the early days of the Reconstruction. A confirmed bachelor and admittedly a terrible cook, he regularly repasts at the **Starlite Diner** (opposite). He is also sweet on Maude Turner who works there, and he hopes to one day to work up the courage to ask her out.

### **thomas Cline,** _**age 43**_ **,** _**council Vice-president**_

- **Description:** stocky, with a thin mustache; frequently wears a tailored suit.

Cline is a shrewd lawyer and an influential member of the **Sons of Seth** (below)—he becomes cult leader following Colonel Grimm’s death in **Box of Evil** (page 88). Cline has council president Nathan Booker wrapped around his finger and operates the group from behind the scenes.

- **Traits:** cheerful, always keen to shake hands.
- **Roleplaying hooks:** the scout-investigators might contact Cline to ask about the Sons of Seth; he feigns ignorance in a rather convincing manner. His daughter, Loretta, belongs to **The Stumbling Moose Squad** (page 65).


### Sons of Seth

The Sons of Seth are quite secretive. While the date of their founding is a mystery to outsiders, the group is extremely old with connections to ancient times. Rumors persist that the group began as a rogue sect of the Knights Templar who came into possession of ancient artifacts and writings.

years of allegiance do members learn the group’s dark secrets and true goals. The Sons of Seth feature heavily in the Westhaven Campaign:

- In **Tremors Below** (page 70), cult member **Thomas Cline** (above) summons a monster to stop Norma Gross exposing the cult.

Members dedicate themselves to the Egyptian god Seth, so Egyptology and its symbols factor heavily in their rituals and ceremonies. The Sons of Seth have temples located across the world. Dozens of temples dot the United States. Typical temples usually have only a few dozen adherents, and they keep their membership small and guarded.

- In Box of Evil (page 88), Thomas Cline uses a different monster, a homunculus, to assassinate the Westhaven Sons of Seth leader, **Colonel Grimm** (page 90). So, Cline replaces Grimm as Westhaven cult leader.

Often, a temple may be hidden in plain sight, in a secret room of a library or embedded within another social club. Most group members remain unaware of any rumored more sinister ideals, and most view the Sons of Seth as a harmless bit of fun for wealthy socialites and a place to network. Some whisper that only after

- The final scenario, **Shadow Over Westhaven** (page 132), sees Cline summon a Mythos deity, the Windwalker. Unless the scout-investigators stop him, Cline intends to lead the worldwide cult!


### Westhaven

### 11. W. Pennyforth Bookseller

### Investigating Cline

The unassuming store front of this used bookstore belies the rich treasures found within. While the store sells new releases, its true value is in the stacks of used books available. The deeper into the store a customer goes, the older and more obscure the volumes become. The breadth of topics covered truly impresses—enabling Keepers to use the bookstore as a potential research site.

Cline’s name crops up in the first two scenarios:

- Norma Gross suspects that Cline has it in for her and summoned the monsters in **Tremors Below** (page 70).
- Cline delivers the box to Colonel Grimm; he also arrives at the end of **Box of Evil** (page 88).

The bookstore owner is **Walter Pennyforth** (below). This older gentleman, originally from London, England, is a curious fellow. He is quiet and standoffish to most, which gives him a reputation as being a snob among the locals. This stems from his impatience with people who ask stupid questions. He makes time for those who appreciate the knowledge contained in his old books and those with a thirst for learning.

The scout-investigators may therefore wish to find out more about Cline—this should not be easy. Cline is quite wealthy; he usually has staff around him, including a driver and personal assistant. After Colonel Grimm’s demise in **Box of Evil** , Cline takes over the Sons of Seth, granting him additional resources and support from cult members.

The bookshop receives frequent visits from several important people within the scenarios, including **Dr. Deanna Bartlett** (page 57), **Colonel Grimm** (page 90), and **Boxcar Jim** (page 62).

Should scout-investigators ask around about Cline, people encourage them not to get involved with “grown-up business.” If they persist, the Keeper can make it difficult, but should not block the players. Ultimately, if Cline is somehow eliminated, replace him with another NPC in the final scenario, **Shadow Over Westhaven** (page 132).

Notable NPCs: Walter Pennyforth (below), Dr. Deanna Bartlett (page 57), **Colonel Joesef Grimm** (page 90), **Boxcar Jim** (page 62).


### **walter Pennyforth,** _**age 68**_ **,** _**used Book Purveyor**_

Pennyforth is an odd gentleman who dislikes timewasters. The rarity and depth of his books make the bookstore a treasure trove of potential research help and story plots. Through the years, reading obscure tomes that have passed his way, Pennyforth has picked up some Cthulhu Mythos knowledge and two spells. However, he doesn’t currently believe the spells work. While most likely an ally for scout-investigators, he could potentially become a villain—perhaps he becomes a little power-crazed on discovering his spells work.

Greek 40%, Latin 50%. _**Spells**_ Chant of Thoth, Dominate (see **Appendix C: Spells** , page 170).

Brawl 40% (20/8), damage 1D3, or dagger 1D4+1 Dodge 40% (20/8)

- **Traits:** an introvert, he often ignores people he doesn’t wish to engage, preferring to read. He tends to glare over his spectacles at people, making him look disturbed by their presence.

### **boxcar Jim,** _**age 44**_ **,** _**transient & Friend**_


Boxcar Jim is a strange fellow. He appears to be a destitute “hobo” (vagrant). However, his shabby exterior hides a man who seems to have lived several lifetimes and seen truly bizarre sights on his adventures.

Boxcar Jim was born James Cavendish in Tombstone, Arizona. He grew up on a hog farm, getting up early, doing chores, and then going to school. Jim dreamed of seeing the far-off places he read about at school.

Jim left at 16, deciding to travel the world and see those wondrous far-off, exotic places. He started by hitching rides on rail cars and traveling across the US, ultimately making his way to New York City. From there, Jim scraped up enough money for steerage passage to Europe.

In 1901, Jim took a steam ship from southern France to western Africa, docking in Dakar in what is now Senegal. From there, he wandered down the western coast, making his way to present day South Africa. Jim had exhausted all his money when he decided to sign up as a mercenary to fight in the Second Boer War. This decision would impact him deeply.

Boxcar Jim‘s nickname comes from his method of traveling around the region in the boxcars of freight trains. He typically stays in a town until he can no longer get supplies or the townsfolk tire of him. This usually lasts a couple of weeks, sometimes longer. He might be gone for months, but eventually returns to Westhaven and repeats the cycle.

### _**combat**_

Brawl 40% (20/8), damage 1D3 Dodge 40% (20/8)

### _**skills**_

Art/Craft (Cooking) 40%, Credit Rating 02%, Cthulhu Mythos 15%, History 60%, Natural World 70%, Occult 60%, Persuade 50%, Psychology 50%, Spot Hidden 80%, Stealth 45%, Throw 70%.

His wild, unkempt looks and sometimes nonsensical ramblings cause most adults to steer clear, adding to his loneliness. True, he makes little sense when recounting strange stories about mystical colors or strange creatures from the stars. Yet, if scoutinvestigators befriend Jim and help him with food, clothing, or money he is an invaluable resource to the adventurers; he can perhaps even provide the junior investigators means to survive their coming troubles.

### _**spells**_

Banish the Visitor, Bless Blade, Immolate (nearby).

- **Description:** tall, scruffy looking with wild sandyblond hair and a bushy beard, all streaked with gray; his face carries numerous wrinkles, creases, and scars. Appears older than his true age due to his hard life. His dirty clothes are full of holes, and he is always looking for replacements.

Jim also possesses low-level psychic abilities. He frequently dreams visions that may come to pass, although they are often disjointed and confusing.

- **Traits:** a smile dances on his lips when he talks to the scout-investigators; a kind person, sees much of himself in the youths.

**Note:** if preferred, with a few backstory changes, Boxcar Jim can be Boxcar Jane.

- **Roleplaying hooks:** Jim makes an ideal NPC for tying together this book’s various tales. He can also seed stories and move the plot along as needed. He also owns a collection that he passes on to the scout-investigators over the course of the scenarios (see **Boxcar Jim‘s Useful Items** , opposite).

STR 60 CON 50 SIZ 60 DEX 80 INT 70 APP 40 POW 70 EDU 40 SAN 30 HP 11 DB: 0 Build: 0 Move: 7 MP: 14 Luck:—
Jim carries all his possessions in a tattered canvas backpack. This includes a bedroll, a well-worn Bowie knife in a cracked leather scabbard, a tin cup, a half-full flask of whiskey, his spoon, a moth-eaten sweater (for cold weather), and many trinkets. Also, Jim has some interesting, powerful items in there. Along with numerous occasionally confusing travel journals, he has the following magic spells written down.

_of Cthulhu: Keeper Rulebook,_ page 246 _)._ Should there be fewer than three scout-investigators, the Keeper may ignore the three-caster requirement. Also, the “physical representation” can be as basic as a charcoal drawing or crude stick figure.

### Bless Blade

- Cost: 5 POW; 10 Luck points*

- **Casting time:** 1 hour

> _***Note:** Sanity point costs for these spells have been replaced with a Luck point cost; Sanity is not used by scout-investigators in_ Campfire Tales _. If being cast by NPCs, revert Luck to Sanity for casting purposes._

### Banish the Visitor

- Cost: 20 magic points; 20 Luck points* (both costs to be shared by the casters)

- **Casting Time:** 1 hour

This spell can trap a trans-dimensional human or human-like intelligence who traveled here from another dimension. It also works on creatures who should no longer be here, such as ghosts. It fails against creatures commanded by another intelligence.

At least three people must participate in the casting. All participants must know the spell. More participants increase the effectiveness. This spell is powered by destroying, with ritual flames, a physical representation of the target (like a voodoo doll); and it requires a glass bottle with a cork stopper.

The spell must be cast in a place of power associated with the target; this could be known haunts, its last known location, or its point of entry into this dimension. For the spell to take effect, win an opposed POW roll between the caster with the highest POW and the target. Certain bonuses can be gained to the casting (maximum of two):

- Grant one bonus die for casting in the middle of the night (12–3 am).
- Grant one bonus die if the caster possesses something that belonged to the target (e.g., hair, fingernails, bones, personal journal, or their jewelry).
- Grant one bonus die if doubling the magic and Luck point expenditure (a total combined cost to the casters of 40 magic points and 40 Luck points).

Casters must divide their numbers: at least one must remain outside a protective circle while the rest stand within. The spell’s target cannot enter the protective circle during casting. If the spell succeeds, the target gets trapped in the bottle, provided the bottle remains unbroken and the cork stopper in place.

Jim acquired this spell during the Second Boer War. It is written on a rolled-up parchment and stuffed into a small glass bottle with a cork stopper.

**Note:** a variant of the spell Banishment of Yde Etad (see _Call_ This spell creates a blade capable of damaging or killing entities that cannot be harmed by mundane weapons. It requires a blood sacrifice of an animal at least SIZ 50. It is written on a weathered, oiled canvas, folded multiple times and bound by twine.

The knife blade must be elemental metal, such as iron or silver. The blade may be any size; however, larger blades do greater damage, but also count as **Cumbersome Weapons** (page 40). If the blade is broken, melted, or otherwise damaged, it permanently loses this ability; however, the blade is never harmed when attacking supernatural entities.

While traveling in Spain, Jim visited a Templar castle. He had been curious about the mysterious holy knights since childhood. At the castle, Jim spent much time transcribing carvings on old statues and ceremonial stone blocks. He made etchings on paper and took all his notes with him.

He later realized he accidentally copied down a powerful Templar spell. It came in handy later when he had to escape extra-dimensional beings during the Second Boer War.

### Immolate

• Cost: 5 magic points; 10 **Luck** points*

- **Casting time:** 1 round

Reciting this spell and flinging a pinch of ash into the air, produces a torch-size ball of flame. This ball erupts on the targeted creature or an object within 30 feet (9 m) of the caster. Anything flammable immediately ignites and burns until extinguished, causing 1D6 damage at the end of each round. It takes a full action to douse the flames. If nothing is flammable (e.g., a stone statue) the flame dies.

Jim found this spell while rummaging through the trash cans outside **Mortimer’s** (page 59) one evening. As he dined on the remains of a pork chop, he observed two well-dressed gentlemen smoking outside as they left the restaurant. One handed the other a cocktail napkin, which then went in the pocket of the other man’s suit jacket.

As they parted ways, the man reached into his jacket for matches to light his cigarette, causing the folded napkin to fall to the ground. Unaware of this, the man walked briskly into the night. The napkin blew over to Jim, who picked it up and read it. Realizing the words’ importance, he stuffed it into his pocket for later.

Initially, this spell comes on a crumpled-up cocktail napkin, but scout-investigators may transcribe it onto something sturdier.

### Chapter 3

### 15. Smith Bros. Salvage

### 12. H. H. Bottoms Library

The town library is a popular destination for local youth. The library has numerous works, including the recently published _The Wolf Cub’s Handbook_ (1916) written by Robert Baden-Powell (founder of The Boy Scouts Association in the UK). Many works of fiction engaging to young people populate the shelves, including _The Wind in the Willows_ (Kenneth Grahame, 1908) and _Little Wizard Stories of Oz_ (L. Frank Baum, 1914).

### 13. Westhaven Historical Society

The historical society is a modest establishment staffed by volunteers, notably old Mrs. Abigail Wolfe and her sister, Mildred. The building houses an extensive collection of local periodicals and ephemera.

The metal salvage yard belongs to Daniel “Rusty” Smith. Rusty’s nickname comes from all the rusted metal in the salvage yard rather than red hair (he is bald). Rusty and his brother, Carl, started the yard while young and have been operating it for over 20 years. Unfortunately, Carl died four years ago when a stack of metal fell on him. Curiously, Rusty’s story changes every time anyone asks about his brother’s death—the truth is they were both drunk, and Rusty is wracked with guilt.

The salvage yard is a place where scout-investigators can go to explore; they can sit in all the cool, old cars; get advice from Rusty (the “wizard of Westhaven“ as he calls himself); or sell salvage for cash to spend at **Harley’s Sweet Shoppe** (page 56).

### 16. Westhaven Area High School

### 14. Henderson’s Garage

Henderson’s fix-it shop works on all manner of motor vehicles, from cars to tractors. When not too busy, Henderson also services bicycles for local children for a small fee.

Westhaven Area High School is home of the Frothy Raccoons, and its school colors are red, black, and gray. The regional high school also serves smaller towns surrounding Westhaven, with an enrollment of 325. The school competes in a variety of sports, including baseball, basketball, cross country, football, track, and
wrestling. Although no powerhouse, it has seen some successful seasons recently. Some scout-investigators most likely participate in sports and other clubs here.

**Notable NPC: Paul Glenn** (page 56).

### 17. Colonel Grimm’s House

A stately, two-story, colonial-style house. This location features in **Box of Evil** (page 88).

Notable NPCs: Colonel Josef Grimm (page 90), and **Evelyn Habersham** (page 90).

### 18. Gustaf’s Farm

A large, rundown old house on the south side of town. Gustaf and his sister, Sydney, and this house feature prominently in the final scenario, **Shadow Over Westhaven** (page 132). They raise pigs to sell for pork and sausages at the local market.

Notable NPCs: Gustaf Brumsted (page 134), and **Sydney Brumsted** (page 135).

Each Stumbling Moose is detailed below; they are all 11 years old at the start of the first scenario. Each time the scoutinvestigators go up to the next scouting rank, the Keeper should make commensurate changes for The Stumbling Mooses.

She hates the idea of camping outdoors.

### Arkham Option

If desired, Keepers can use Chaosium’s _Arkham_ instead of Westhaven as the setting for these scenarios. Few adjustments are needed, just rename the Westhaven Wayfarer Scouts to the Arkham Wayfarer Scouts. Arkham equivalents for all Westhaven locations are below. Alternatively, relocate the Westhaven locations to unpopulated buildings in Arkham.

All Westhaven NPCs can easily be relocated to their Arkham equivalent locations. New characters like Boxcar Jim and the Stumbling Moose Squad can simply be dropped into Arkham appropriate places. Alternatively, have scout-investigators grow up in Westhaven, then move to Arkham when they reach adulthood and become fully fledged investigators.
